NO! NO! 

THE WOMAN By NORMAN KLEIN 

In presenting this first book of a new writer, we believe that we are introducing an author as distinctly original as 

S. S. Van Dine and Dashiell Hammett. "No! No! The Wo man" is a tale of murder, mystery and wild adventure.

From its opening chapter, which describes the peculiar funeral of the rich and eccentric recluse, Miss Pollard, in her haunted Fifth Avenue 

Palace, to the terrific fight to the death on board a dingy cabin cruiser in the Sound, the book is a seething mass of plots and sub-plots teeming with richly portrayed characters. We have not previously met such a delightful detective as the thoroughly beautiful and thoroughly hard-boiled Leslie Cobb.

Both the characters and the incidents in 

"No! No! The Woman!" are entirely fic tional; they are not borrowed from life, and there is no intention of suggesting that This a/o That actually might have happened.

THE WOMAN!

CHAPTER I  

Monday A. M., June 1st

MISS PLUNKETT led the man with the black case upstairs. 

Oil portraits in gold frames hung on the landing and in the hall on the second floor. The hall was lighted but the electric lamps were so yellow and frugal that Miss Plunkett and the man with the black case seemed held in a trap of shadows. "Come this way, please Miss Plunkett paused a broken moment in front of a heavy walnut door on the second floor; she touched the hollow in her thin throat. A New Englander of sixtythree, she was tall and strong, with a greyish complexion and flat cheeks. From her eyes wrinkles ran down to the corners of her mouth, the scars of ancient tears. 

Her white, thin lips struggled. "Miss Pollard is in here." Her whisper was harsh, almost bitter. "Well," said the man, "if you left her here, she's still here."

He was a lean man and his between the wings of his collar. 

Adam's apple showed 

His name was Ellwell 

Bostwick Yeatman. He was from the Causwell Funeral

Establishments.

He followed Miss Plunkett into the large old-fashioned bedroom with its high ceiling. Brown shades and velvet drapes were drawn at the three windows. The room smelled of rubber hot-water bottles. 

Miss Plunkett pushed the electric light button near the door. She stopped as if hypnotized by a sight which was by now familiar to her, then went toward the great mahogany bed, turned her back to it and faced Mr. Yeatman. "Are-are you prepared to―?” "Oh, yes," he said; "you have to be, in this business."

As Miss Plunkett stepped to the head of the bed, Mr. 

Yeatman said: "She looks splendid. She looks very splendid, Mrs. Ug." "Miss Plunkett. I am rather, I was-her personal companion and secretary for thirty-seven years.' "Ah, that's a long time. It must be a great loss, Miss Plunkett." his She drew away as if afraid he was going to put hand on her. Standing at the bed watching him, she reached out and touched the edge of the pillow. 

He asked, "When did she pass away?" "Early," said Miss Plunkett. 

He was biting a fingernail. "And was the demise peaceful-without pain?" "Yes... yes."

Mr. Yeatman nodded toward the dead woman in the bed, tried another nail. "She certainly had what it takes. 

I bet she had enough to make Henry Ford lay awake nights." He asked, "Suppose you'll be well taken care of? Thirty-seven years, you said? Some people have all the luck." 

She asked sharply: "What's that?" "She-ah-treated you pretty good?"

Suddenly Miss Plunkett's virginal old face darkened. "I've-a- " She seemed dazed in thought; she glanced past Mr. Yeatman to the curtained and shaded windows that shut out Fifth Avenue. "-I've already done her hair. It is the way she wanted it. I know how she wanted it." “Madam, it is necessary, if we are to do the job right." "But I say it is not. I've done it." "Still, a little combing- "9 "I tell you-you don't need bother with her hair." "No? Why not?"

The room was very large, very quiet; active with that hot-water bottle odor. She said: "It is not at all necessary." Her old fingers plucked at her mouth.

Suddenly Mr. Yeatman reached for her arm. "That's right," he said, "sit down there now. Now, don't faint.” 

Miss Plunkett said, "Leave me be." "Maybe you'd rather go downstairs while I-" 

Her eyes lighted shrewdly. "No, I will stay right here."

Mr. Yeatman opened his case and busied himself with the gruesome apparatus of his trade. "Maybe I could get you a glass of water?" 

Miss Plunkett gazed at the dead woman's face, shuddered. "What did you say?" "A drink?”

The old woman stared at him, said nothing. He took out several bottles with white labels on them, and held up a rouge pot. "I won't be long," he said. "See this? I'm going to touch up her cheeks... oh, not much. At her age, not much. But just a weeney bit, you know- 

The woman's fingers tightened white with rage.

"Don't you touch her! I tell you...." She jumped up, hovered over him. "Don't you dare touch her." Twice she tried to speak. "I tell you...." Then her mood fled across her face. With difficulty she said: "Perhaps, sir, it is you who feel the need of a drink?"

Mr. Yeatman stood up from his litter on the floor. 

He adjusted the blue and white four-in-hand beneath his quavering Adam's apple. "Me?" he asked. "Me?"

Miss Plunkett smiled with her secret plan. She moved actively-the jerky gait of the strong old New England woman. At a tall door beside a mahogany highboy she turned, relieved to find the man had not moved. "You wait there!" Her eyes carried past him to the bed. 

Mr. Yeatman grinned.

When she opened the door he saw a closet and on a shelf some seven or eight bottles. He seemed about to whistle.

Muttering to herself, Miss Plunkett returned with a half-used bottle of Old Grandad rye and a glass. "Will this suit you? There is a little red wine here too and some kümmel." 

Mr. Yeatman was kittenish. "I very seldom touch it.” 

She thrust the glass and bottle at him. . . . "Oops," he said, "look what I've done! Spilled right over the carpet." "Never mind." "Well-!" He turned around toward the bed, his chin bent over the glass. "May sweet dreams and a beautiful rest come to her-Sophia Pollard." He threw back his head, drank, added: "Last of the Pollards." While touching his mouth with a handkerchief from his breast pocket he said: "Say! That's right, isn't it? The last of them. Yes sir."

-More?" 

Mr. Yeatman looked at the bottle in her hand. "That is the real old stuff all right. But no; no thank you, I have to get the remains ready, I thank you very kindly."

She filled his glass. He threw back his head and drank. 

She waited. He held his handkerchief to his lips, coughed lightly. "Try some more?" "Well, just a drop. When!" "What?" "I said 'When!' but there you've gone and filled her full, I really didn't want to- "Never mind." 

She nodded toward the door leading to the hall and big stairway. Her voice was stony, like the farms of her birthplace. "None of them will come up here, don't you worry about them. Not that tribe.... Leslie Cobb would of.

But she's restless as the sea of Galilee; she's scattered off. But you won't see them here; oh, no." Her pink, beakish nose drew back sniffing. "Not them!" "Yeh?" Mr. Yeatman was reading the label. "By 

George, I'll say that is old stuff. Real Old Grandad." 

Nodding toward the closet: "All those bottles! Did she like it? You know." 

Miss Plunkett was furious. "What?" "You know. The schnapps. Did she—?” 

Miss Plunkett rolled the cork in her fingers. "Her blood was getting thin.... Rarely, very rarely, on winter nights." 

Mr. Yeatman felt he should console her. "Pardon me!" he said and offered the glass. "Never touched it in my whole life! Never." 1

"Well," he said, "don't bite my nose off. A drop (especially pre-war like that there) soothes the nerves." "I guess you want some water?" 

He stepped toward her. "Now, don't you bother about that. I'll take it natural, like a babe with its mama. This is quite refreshing as it is. Very refreshing. I was quite tuckered out when I got the call from the office, you don't know how hard our work is, they had me on tick until 11:30 last night. Client over on Park Avenue, a

Mr. Latshaw. Big radio network man. Sick for years, somebody said. Quite in the dough from the looks of the establishment." He leaned near her and she wrinkled her nose. He pointed his glass at the woman lying neatly clothed in bed. "And here's Sophia Pollard! 

Funny. I've read how she lived like a hermit..

No foolin', exactly how many millions did she have? 

Go on. I won't tell a soul." ... 

Miss Plunkett's upper lip moved. "How d' I know?” "See the will? I suppose you have, who's going to get it all? All those scads and scads. Oh, Lord, what a pity!" 

She said through her teeth, "Have some more?" "Um... well." He drank and said: "Gee whillikins, I thought you were going to hit me with that bottle, before. I wasn't trying to make 

Her eyes bored into him. "I know," she said. “Now come on! One more. It's almost gone." you sore." up He let her pour more into the glass, which he held to the faint light creeping through the windows. In spite of double glass, the traffic of motor-cars and Fifth Avenue busses reached them vehemently. He was blinking but his eyes were fairly steady. "Nice," he said and downed the drink, murmuring "Wah-h, lovely," and wiped his mouth with the back of his hand.

She was watching him. He walked with care toward a bureau. It held a carved ivory Buddha, a heavy gold set of toilet articles, a cut-glass dish containing pins and a thimble, a can of Mennen's baby talcum, and a silver dish in which was a mustard-colored tin of crystallized ginger.

He put down his glass. "I saw in The New Yorkeror was it in the Graphic? No, I'm sure now it was The 

New Yorker-where she slipped off to one of those Broadway joints and she liked some little twitchet so much she sent her a $2,500 string of pearls. Come on, tell me, was that just the hooey? The old schmoos?" 

But Miss Plunkett said-"Wait!" Her tall spare figure moved to the closet. She came back holding a bottle against her side. "Well, now- " he began. "Take it! You might as well have it. You need not say-need not say to anyone, you understand?-that I gave this to you.' you. 

“Well, now." He had a pretty good edge on. "'S'nice of What is it? Whew! Genuine old cog-nack, the old French cog-nack, that's a treat. Much 'bliged, am sure. I'll take it home for the wife_33 "Drink it now." "Ha, think I'm a fish?"

He stowed the bottle under some gauze in his black case and when he stood up his face was colored. In turning toward the dead woman in the bed he reeled. He looked slyly at Miss Plunkett and put a thumb and bent forefinger upon the bed.

Miss Plunkett had come beside him. She watched him narrowly. The light from the chandelier reflected in her glasses, giving her eyes a brilliance.

Mr. Yeatman took off his coat, turned back his cuffs. 

His grin was wet. "Well," he said, "and now to work. 

It won't take but a jiffy." 

She watched him. 

Gavin, the muscular young Irishman, who, with his wife Mary, was caretaker in the morbid old Fifth Avenue house, returned from the front door. He spoke to John 

Phelps. "It's a reporter, he says. Desires to see some one, sir." 

John Phelps said, "Eh? Reporter?" He was about forty-eight or fifty, well-tailored, substantial-looking; a meaty, blond-headed man with hulking shoulders and mild eyes. He was a figure in that hectic and complicated phase of the oil business-speculating in Oklahoma, Texas and Kansas oil well royalty rights.

He walked several steps and returned to Neville Phelps. "The devil," he said. 

Neville Phelps opened a cigarette case. "Reporter, eh?" "Why can't he see Whittle? Those fellows ought to know better than bother us at such a time." 

Without looking up Neville Phelps said: “Gavin, tell him to see Whittle." 

Neville was slender, not as big-boned as his brother

John; but he was handsomer and darker. His Indian features once inspired a platinum-blonde from an Earl 

Carroll show to ask: "Are you one of them, baby? I like Jew boys. They're so generous and brutal." His face and hands were sensitive and there were rather interesting pouches under his eyes.

John Phelps walked up and down. "Well, then, where is Whittle? How long does it take to come in from 

Montclair ?"

Eleanor Duane called from the next room: "What is it, John?" She had a drawling voice that often soared out of control. "Who is out there?" Her eyes were swollen. She sat with Margery Phelps, Neville's wife. 

Neville said: "Reporter." "John, I think you ought to talk to him. They maake things up, if you don't." "We don't want any publicity." you "Neville! How silly. I never he-ard anything so silly in my life. Don't re-alize Aunt is dead upstairs? How can you stop that publicity? I learned how those reporters cook up things when I was on that Republican workers' committee- "Good God, Eleanor. This isn't a political event." • · 

She called to the hall: "Gavin! Tell the young man to come in. John, you're the oldest; you're head of the family now. Give him the necessary facts, I tell you!"

The reporter from the Sun was lanky and stoopshouldered. He looked about through glasses with large gold frames. He seemed excited, though slightly ill from being in a house with a corpse. "Er-is Miss Sophia Pollard dead?" 

John Phelps said, "Yes." "That's what our man at the County Building said; he got the tip from the Health Department offices. I live right over on East Sixty-first and so the office called me and I shot right over." 

Margery Phelps motioned to Eleanor Duane as if she would enjoy wrapping her fingers around his throat. "What do you want to know?" John Phelps asked.

The Sun man took out some copy paper folded twice. "When did Miss Pollard pass away?"

99 "About six this morning. She became slightly indisposed earlier and her companion, Miss Plunkett- "What's her first name?" 

John Phelps began to get angry. "Who, the companion?" He was newly shaved and the muscles moved in his shiny jaws. "That's of no consequence. Just say 'Miss Plunkett."" "We have to have her Christian name." 

John Phelps turned to Neville. 66 -It's Lucy," drawled Eleanor Duane from the next room. "John! He has to haave those things." "Was she ill?" 

John Phelps put his right hand to his chin. Without turning his head his eyes watched Neville. He cleared his throat. "No, she was not. Miss Plunkett phoned Aunt 

Sophia's physician right away-phoned all of us. But she was gone before any of us arrived." "What is the physician's name, please?" 

John Phelps turned to Mrs. Duane-she nodded-he said: "Dr. Spoors. He has been attending Aunt Sophia for year." He watched the Sun man write and added: "Raymond Collet Spoors."

Gavin, the caretaker, was in the doorway. "There are three reporters here, sir, and they- 99 

John Phelps waved his hand. "Tell them to wait!" 

He nodded to the Sun man-"Anything else?" His forehead glistened. "John, de-ar, don't do that," Mrs. Duane called. "You'll only have to repeat everything-I know! Bring them in, have it over with." "All right,” he said.

Reporters from the Times, World-Telegram and Eve-

ning Post came in, ranged themselves alongside the Sun man, pulled copy paper out of their pockets. 

Mrs. Duane recognized one of the men. He was one of the morning callers at that Republican Club up on Madison Avenue. He carried a malacca walking stick on his forearm and wore spats. When he turned aroundto see the Sun man's notes-she smiled. His trouser seat was worn on the left cheek. 

The World-Telegram man broke in: "Exactly how old was she? Why did she never marry? Did she have a 

Ten Commandments for Happiness?" "Let's see," John said; "she was eighty-four." "And-uh-the will? Can you estimate for us how many millions she leaves?" 

John Phelps looked as if he were going to strike him. 

But he said: "Man, this is no time to ask such questions." "Well-she's last of the Pollards. Right?" 

John Phelps stared heavily and after a moment he said: "Yes. I suppose that's right. There was her father,

Elijah Agnew Pollard. He bought real estate when it still was farms- 

"I know," the reporter said. "And Elijah left the estate to his children, Stella, David, Calvin and of course-Sophia Pollard. Now they are all dead." He swallowed, looked at the ceiling. "That's all." He rubbed his wet brow with the back of his right hand. "But there are heirs?" 

Neville broke in: "Four children." 

John took it up. "Why, yes. Of course!" He added, Nodding backward toward Eleanor

Mrs. Neville Phelps. What? Yes, Mrs. Eleanor Phelps

Duane. That's right. And Neville here, and myself.

He turned toward the hall. Reporters from the city news, Journal, Brooklyn Eagle, Graphic, Herald

Tribune, American and Mirror now appeared; also the 

New York correspondent of the Chicago Daily News. 

They swiped notes over the other men's shoulders. "What time did she" 66 -Hadn't been sick, had she?” "Three heirs, eh?"

John Phelps moved his mouth as if he had a food sliver in a tooth. "No," he said, "four." "But," said the scholarly Times man, "there's you, Mr. 

Phelps. And Mr. Neville Phelps, here; and Mrs. Duane. 

That only makes three." 

John Phelps parted his mustache with thumb and forefinger. "Right. The other is Leslie Cobb." "Will he be here?" 

Neville said: "She. Leslie Phelps Varden Cobb." 

John Phelps smiled a bit. "I don't think so. The last we-I, at least-heard about her she was flying in some hunting party, some Lady-or-Other's plane. Flew from 

Croydon to Africa. There was a short squib about it in your paper, Trib." 

Neville Phelps answered one man: "No, we aren't related to her by blood... I thought you newspaper fellows knew all about that. Aunt Sophia was our great-aunt. Right. "John here, Eleanor and I-Leslie Cobb too-were the adopted children of John Marshall Phelps. Of his first marriage. Later Father married Cherry Pollard, daughter of Calvin Pollard, brother of"-he nodded toward the ceiling-"Aunt Sophia. Guess it's mixed up for you?"

Somebody asked: "Leslie Cobb's married?"

John Phelps looked over the questioner's head toward the windows. "Twice," he said. "We haven't seen her for three-four years. She's been abroad and-" 

The city news man was impatient. "Pardon me. I just want to ask one question-then I got a deadline to catch. When's the funeral?”

A large man, John Phelps perspired easily. He drew out a handkerchief, using his right hand. His left arm seemed disused, limp. "Tomorrow at three," he said. "Aunt-lived very quietly as you know. The services will be very quiet.

We don't want you to make a big to-do over it." He seemed to dislike the lot of them. 

There was a clatter in the doorway. A man with dangling feet in braces came forward on his crutches; but aside from his legs he was robust, well set-up. He was around sixty and had a fine face and close-cut grey hair. He fumbled with a brief-case and a grey hat with white piping. "Whittle!" John Phelps said. "Do come over here.

You know how to handle these newspaper boys better than we." "Hello, Judge Whittle," the Journal man said.

The lawyer deposited his crutches and sat down stiffly. 

He wiped his broad yellow forehead. 

At this moment Gavin spoke to Neville Phelps, who was lighting a cigarette from the Herald Tribune man's lighter. 

John Phelps heard Gavin. "We'll not have that!" he said. "That's a bit too much." Gavin nodded, started for the door. "No pictures!" 

The lawyer Whittle growled, "Of course not. Leave

the photogs to me." He said to the reporters: "Why don't you boys come over to my office? It's two blocks away. These people are very much disturbed. You can use my phones. I'll give you everything you want.... 

Tuesday P. M., June 2nd "She could of got a million smackers for that old house," a taxi driver said. "But she nixed it. The papers say they tried to buy it for a skyscraper but she nixed it. 

Listen, they can have my house any day for a million smackers." He re-discovered his nose. "No foolin'," the young policeman smiled. "And I'll throw in this yellow chisel-wagon." "Yeah?" "Because, look," the taximan said, "I won't need any chisel-wagon when they hand me a million smackers-" "You better roll," the young policeman said. "The

Captain says we're going to clear this block for the funeral." "Oh, yeh?" The taxi driver looked at the brownshaded house across the street. "It looks like a hot funeral all right." He started down Fifth Avenue. 

The sidewalk in front of Sophia Pollard's "House of

Mystery" was clustering with men and women. Directly opposite the house another crowd surrounded a movietone truck. A sound camera sprawled on the truck's roof. The sky was overcast, villainous with clouds. Now and then a newspaper flashlight went off. 

A man said: "They say she always had one of those

German dachshunds around her. When they died of old age she got another. She called every one 'Otto.' 

Funny, eh?" 

Cf. Pages 1, all June 2nd editions, New York City newspapers; Time, the weekly newsmagazine, June 8th.

"Many times," somebody said, "I used to see her come out for a ride in her carritch."

The stone stairway area leading to the house was held by four policemen. Women shoppers, men, girls, messenger boys, chauffeurs crowded up. Their faces had that half-amused, curious, idiotic expression which they later identified joyously in the newspaper halftones. 

The Pollard house stood in the middle of the block on the east side of the Avenue. Exclusive shops and department stores elbowed up on both sides; but the 1884 Pollard house, with its brick wall and rusted iron spikes, demanded, and held, the respect due old age, and shaded mystery.

Neville Phelps arrived with his wife Margery. The camera men went into action. Mrs. Phelps was shorter than her husband. She was a beautiful small woman. 

She had black hair, a small pointed nose, small mouth, small feet. As she crossed the sidewalk and mounted the steps she managed to look at the spectators under her sorrowing brows. She had heavy eyebrows. She used a depilatory on her arms and legs. 

Then Mrs. Whittle, senior, stepped out of a limousine. 

She and the chauffeur helped her crippled husband.

They were followed by their son, Titus Ricks Whittle 

Jr., and Mrs. Whittle. "That's distant relatives-wish I were in their shoes," a woman said. The elder Whittle frowned, led his wife up the steps. "You'll have to keep back!" a policeman said. Another said: "Everybody back."

Next John Phelps stepped out of an Auburn. It had a

low greyish-tan top. He helped his wife Ann. John 

Phelps Jr., seventeen, followed.

Inside, the Causwell men were in charge. Causwell himself was generalissimo. Whiskey always gave him the proper melancholy look for these big occasions. . . . He was a pal, he said, of camera men, hadn't he always given them a break? Hadn't he set up a press bar in the hotel room across the street from his place for every Page One funeral held from there? Now, boys, give these Phelpses a break, please! Don't clutter the hall with all that camera equipment. Please!

The casket rested on a stand in the front drawing room. There were many flowers. Thirty-five or forty people stood about. Some were the servants brought in from Sophia Pollard's estate in Stamford, Connecticut.

They stood out because they were awkardly dressed and awkwardly weeping. Several others were from philanthropic organizations. Sophia Pollard had given to many charities but her donations seldom reached the papers. "There's Mr. Whittle!" whispered Mr. Andreas of the

Protestant Episcopal fund. 

"I suppose," whispered Miss Turnbull of the Charity 

Organization Society, "that he drew up the will?" 

Mr. Andreas coughed behind his hand.

In the rear parlor Miss Plunkett sat by a window and stared at the coach house in the rear. Mary Gavin, the caretaker's wife, moved about snuffling. Gavin was dressed as if prepared to serve dinner.

Eleanor Duane, widow, sat on a petit-point sofa with her daughter Francine, who was sixteen, full-figured, blond and sensuous-eyed. Francine squeezed her hand. "You are a brick, Mother."

Mrs. Duane wept again. "It's all so sa-ad," and she sighed. The sigh broke in halves in her throat. 

The Rev. Dr. Eliot Farquhar James came downstairs from the W. C. In the hall he was met by his assistant, Dr. Tousley, who said: "We should be ready, sir, in a short while." "I am ready," the Rev. Dr. James said. He walked through the drawing room, passed the casket and flowers, and found John Phelps in the library, standing very still, looking at a shelf holding the Louisa M. Alcott series. "It is almost three," the Rev. Dr. James said. "Make it brief, for God's-uh, excuse me. We're under such a heinous strain, Dr. James." put

The clergyman came very close to him and hand on his left shoulder-John drew away wincing. "It's a boil on my arm," he explained. his "Try to buck up, old man," the Rev. Dr. James said. "It's hard, very hard. My heart goes out to you to all of you grieving children. She was a woman with a beautiful Christian character." 

John Phelps's eyes filled. “Buck up, old man," the Rev. Dr. James said. He was a kind, human, prosperous churchman.

The taxi rolled past the Pulitzer fountain opposite the Hotel Plaza, turned south on Fifth Avenue. Leslie Cobb looked forward at the grim house. "You won't be able to drive up there," she said. The driver seemed nonplussed by the crowd. "I said-don't turn. Just stop here."

The taxi pulled in. The people on the curb stared at 

Leslie Cobb in new French clothes. She wore them as if she were used to new French clothes.

"I'm stopping for the fare to get out," the driver said to a policeman. "I don't know this part so good. I'm from Brooklyn." 

The policeman said, "I'll keep your secret."

Leslie Cobb stood on the curb. She leaned into the cab and embraced a girl of seven. "Kiss me, Vally. You'll be a good girl, won't you? ... Miss Blythe, you'll take 

Valery to the hotel." 

The governess said, "Yes, Mrs. Cobb." 

The little girl watched her mother; she put her face against the taxi window. It made her nose grotesque and white. Leslie Cobb pushed through the crowd. She crossed Fifth Avenue near the Fifty-eighth Street corner. 

She was twenty-eight and tall. Sun and wind had stained her skin as if she'd used the juice of ripe walnut husks. Like her speech her walk was deliberate and quite splendid. As her first husband, Ten Varden, used to say: "Leslie's tail doesn't stick out from her riding pants and her elbows don't stick out from her evening rig."

She brushed her hair back from her forehead because a hairdresser in the rue des Acacias in 1920 told her she had the brow of a goddess.

Looking at her face people were bewildered by her eyes, so silver grey within her black lashes and under her eyebrows that she might have been without sight at all.

At the Pollard steps, Leslie Cobb worked her way through the men and women, went in. Gavin met her. "I'm Mrs. Cobb." "Yes, madam?" He stood there. She walked on and he had to back against a commode. 

Then the elder Whittle saw her, seemed to frown over

£his tortoise-shells. "Mrs. Cobb!" he said. They shook hands. His palm was calloused from grasping a crutch. 

His son joined them. "Hel-lo! Leslie!"

He was thirty-three, Yale, five-foot-nine, nicely dressed, nice-mannered, forging ahead at squash and The Right 

Contacts trustworthy. "It's old Ricks," she said. Then somebody clutched her hand, slobbered. "Eh?" she turned. "Oh, it's David."

The old coachman David, now retired, drooled over her hand. "Twenty-two years I drove her up the Avenue and around the Park on afternoons it didn't look rain." 

The odors of flowers weighted on her. "Good God," she moaned, and walked through wide doors and came upon the casket under its blanket of white roses. Both her hands gripped a silver handle. "Aunt Sophia. oh! Aunt Sophia.” 

Somebody said "What in the world—!" and she turned to face John Phelps. She said, "Hullo, John. I'm in time? Hurried up from the pier. Mauretania. The 

Customs men were so nice about it." 

Neville Phelps approached as John was saying something. "Jeez, it's Les! We thought you were in Africa on safari, Les. We didn't know where to reach you-Aunt died so suddenly." 

Leslie Cobb said slowly, deep in her throat: "She wouldn't be so pleased if she knew I were here." "Let's forget all that now." "During the funeral, John, at any rate." Neither of them met her silver-grey eyes. 

Finally Neville said, "Funny, your coming back like this."

"Read about it yesterday," she said. bulletin printed in the ship's paper. ever saw in one. Where's Eleanor ?" "Back there," Neville said. "There was a radio

It's the only news I John came close to her, his forehead lifted in wrinkles over his nose. Grief made him look rather fine. In a lowered voice he said: "Leslie, you know we are damn glad to see you, don't you? It's a new deal, old fellow."

Then the younger Whittle was at her side again and 

John turned away. "Gee! you look fine," Whittle said. He had nice teeth. "You do too, Ricks. Is Ted here?" 

He stared sullenly at the floor. "Didn't you hear? 

Ted-died last October." "Oh, I am so sorry, Ricks. So awf'ly sorry." 

Eleanor Duane, her daughter Francine, and Ann Phelps stood with Dr. Tousley. Ann in her parched voice said: "Why, it's Leslie. Where did you spring up from?" 

Eleanor Duane's voice hit the ceiling. "We thought you were in Eu-rope, Leslie. Weren't you in Eu-rope, Leslie? 

Oh, Leslie- "and she wept. "I was bringing Valery home. Hullo, Francine. You're so lovely and tall.”

Ann Phelps, who hated her sister-in-law Eleanor's hairtrigger emotions-("dear Eleanor's like Florida weatherrain or shine on a half-moment's notice")—said in a pale way: "How are you, my dear?" "Juust think," Eleanor Duane said, "she went without a bit of pain. Juust-went. Yesterday morning, sleeping." "I'm glad she had no pain." 

Eleanor looked sharply at Leslie Cobb. Then she said: "Oh, Leslie. May I present Dr. Tousley, the new assistant

rector. Dr. Tousley, my sister Mrs. Cobb. She's been flying"--and added weakly, "in Africa." 

Neville's wife Margery came up, kissed her. "Oh, my dear, this is a surprise." "Isn't it?" 

Margery pouted, "Not a very cheerful homecoming," and looked at Leslie Cobb's frock. . . . "Oh," she said, holding out a hand to a black-haired woman with a white face and alizarin-red lips. "May I present the Countess 

Tatiana Pflomm, my house guest? Mrs. Cobb." 

Leslie Cobb looked quietly at the Russian. Only young

Francine Duane seemed to notice traces of a remorseless smile. "How do you do?" Leslie Cobb nodded. 

The Russian showed her teeth. "We will begin the services," Dr. Tousley murmured; "and if you ladies will come with me, please?”

They followed him into the front drawing room. 

Leslie Cobb sat next to John Phelps, whose right hand happened to be on the corner of her chair-back. As he removed his hand he patted her left shoulder. She moved her shoulder in acknowledgment. He leaned near. "There's room in our car." 

She shook her head. "Don't think can do... so terribly hot in New York, after shipboard. May be touch of tropic fever."

Out in the hall young John Phelps Jr. lingered for a last-minute drag on a cigarette. He liked hanging around the camera men. He leaned against the wall beside a camera man who'd come in, opened a black valise and busied himself with a bottle of whitish powder.

John Phelps Jr. flicked his cigarette ash with a little

finger. "Why don't you use those electric flashlamps, like that Daily News fellow?" "Yeh, Daily News!" The camera man was hurriedly sorting a stack of plates in the valise. "They got four guys here. But I got to work it alone. It's a dog's life, brother This stuff? I'm going to pull a big flash when they come out." ...

He bustled toward the front door. John Phelps Jr., left alone, taking a last drag, leaned curiously over the open valise ...

The Rev. Dr. James, in the drawing room, cleared his. throat for quiet. "Our Almighty Father, in his all-seeing wisdom, and graced by the sovereign gentleness and understanding of His Son, has seen fit to summon the soul of our sister, Sophia Pollard, whose beautiful character, whose noble adherence to the tenets of a Christian life, whose boundless charitarian glories we have for so long. "Amen!" slobbered the old coachman. ... 

It seemed there was a flash of light, the Causwell pallbearers grouped waiting in the doorway said later they felt it on their necks, and a thunderous noise, an explosion, and white smoke rolled up the hall, rushed into the front drawing room.

And John Phelps, emerging from the confusion, found his son Jack, face and hands black, clothes burned, blood seeping from an evil gaping wound that left his mouth grinning halfway over into his left cheek. And somebody said "Bomb!" And Ann Phelps, eyes dried by terror, was holding the boy's head against her and saying over and over-"They've killed him, Jack's killed—”

CHAPTER II  LESLI 

ESLIE COBB had difficulty getting out through the sidewalk crowd. She hurried a block east to Madison 

Avenue, crossed, stopped a northbound taxi and told the man to go over to First Avenue. 

At First Avenue she gave the driver a coin, started away.. "Hey!" he yelled. She looked at the coin in his dirt-crusted hand. "I'm sorry," she said. Smiling to herself, she took back the half-crown and gave him a half-dollar. 

At a garage she said: "I used to rent cars here. Do you still rent them? I want a good fast one." "Yes, lady. Have you got your driver's license?” "I forgot. No." "Going far?" "Up into Connecticut-not far." "In a hurry, eh? That might cost a little more for speed. Worth it?"

She said that was all right and he called a man in dark whipcords. The man wheeled out a closed car and filled the gas tank from a red box-shaped wagon just outside the garage office. She saw the manager bring a brass insignia from the office and bolt it to the tie-bars between the front fenders. It was an honorary P. D. sign used by Tammany big-shots. "Stamford," she said.

The chauffeur, swinging into the street, began: “We 25

can make better time by Hutchinson River Parkway as they are fixing the Boston Post Road—” "Any way. So long as you get me there quickly."

After wriggling out of the Bronx, they pushed forward at fifty, sixty. When the motorcycle cops saw the brass 

P. D. sign they nodded, sputtered past. . . . 

Pelham. Port Chester. Speed-trap Greenwich. Cos

Cob, where they curved down a hill just as a red light melted green, to the disappointment of a ruddy-faced policeman in front of a shingled booth. Then up, then down into Stamford. The chauffeur drove around the graceful Town Hall, was halted in traffic opposite Police 

Headquarters. 

"This's Stamford, madam. It's where Gene Tunney lives on a farm. It's very stylish to have a farm around here. I read where-" "Really? Now drive on through Atlantic Squareturn right at the fork away from the Boston Post if 

I remember, there's a fire-engine house . . ." · .. 

Pretty soon the car swung into a road parallel to Long 

Island Sound. "Go on, please, until I tell you." Leslie

Cobb sat forward on the edge of the seat. 

They passed estates, fat and insolent, with green meadows, great white clapboard, hand-hewn shingle or stone houses, white barns, apple orchards, paddocks and white jumping hurdles, stone fences which New England farmers years ago set their eight-dollar-a-month hired hands a-building-“just to keep 'em from loafing." Early flowers blurred the sides of the houses.-At twenty-five now the car moved past these estates. The air was saltcrusted. "Here! Stop here." Leslie Cobb jumped out. "I don't think I'll be very long."

The shoreline here was broken by inlets from a half to two miles wide.

She walked along a California privet hedge which had a stone wall behind it; then came to massive gates. On the left gate wing was a fading sign: Service-North

Drive. In the right gate was a small door. She tried the iron door, it held, she shook it but it held. After a puzzled moment she thrust in her arm. Her fingers found an iron hasp. The door opened at her shove. 

Ten feet inside, by a gravel drive, was the Superintendent's cottage. She walked past it-stopped to return and shut the gate door. At last she was treading through gardens lazy with iris like orchids, scarlet oleanders, tall poppies. On a wall pink and red rambler roses looked as artificially unreal as a Shubert musical comedy set. 

Then a lily pond fed by a brook, a glass flowerhouse, a large white garage amid oak trees. The garage had black hand-forged hardware on the doors.

Along an avenue of poplars she held her suede bag in uneasy fingers. She took deep slow breaths-just as Max 

Reinhardt once at a ship's-concert had prescribed for stage entrances. The sky was a monotone in old pewter. 

Rain promised before night. It was about 4:30. Birds yipped in the trees.

Now she came to a 750-foot length of sand, a sandspit with a road of crushed rock. There was water on both sides now. Wind from the water was chilly on her neck. 

She seemed uneasy, she looked back.

Finally she reached the head of this neck of low-lying sand.... An acre here,-much higher, cooled with oaks and evergreens . . . like an island held in tow of the mainland by the sandspit hawser.

In the trees, on the sea side, stood a two-story Colonial

house, a silent, brooding house, white, with mouldgreen roof.

This "island" retreat stood higher than the surrounding countryside, higher than the listless water fifteen feet below. The water was held back by a concrete wall on the north and east. The south side, too, was abrupt, and faced a wide bay. The bay was deserted as Leslie 

Cobb gazed across it.

She went to the house steps. The front door was locked and around in the back the kitchen door was too. 

She noticed two sloping doors, put her hand under one-it gave. She looked around. A robin stood widelegged, observing her. She stepped inside, groped through the furnace room, climbed some stairs. Something struck her on the head—she fell back startled, and looked up to see a floor beam.

She was opening the door to the butler's pantry-the light through a small window made her squint-and a soft object dashed against her legs and she fell screaming against the door. "Ohh-h-" The object lunged against her again, whined, barked. "You little idiot." She picked it up. "So they didn't take you to her funeral?-Hi, there. Don't eat all the powder off my face, I know you. Your name is Otto, fourteenth edition or so." She held up the dachshund by the shoulders. "Otto!" she mocked at the double row of teats. 

Leslie Cobb opened the refrigerator, found some lamb chops. Otto the dachshund lunched noisily.

Leslie Cobb shut the door, walked on the balls of her feet into the living room, noticed once more the big stone fireplace with its Dutch oven, the tall windows opening

to a stone-flagged terrace, an antique desk. She crossed to this desk, opened it, paused-shut it carelessly. 

Next, Sophia Pollard's bedroom, second floor, overlooking the Sound. And she trembled. Branches of a big oak switched the roof. 

A noise. 

Poised, she whirled, went to the door, waited. Againcreaks. She waited. Then shook her head, as if shaken by a nervous spasm. She walked to the stairway.-"Hello?" 

Her voice trailed down the stairs. Her wrist watch said nearly five o'clock. Hurriedly she turned back to the bedroom with its walnut four-poster. "Still here!"

It was an old chest. hobnail vase of asters. like gypsies' earrings.

It stood by the wall, bearing a 

The drawers had brass handles 

Leslie Cobb jerked at the top drawer at the next-down to the bottom. 

They all held firmly. 

She ran out, fleeing at a run down the stairs, and in the pantry the dog still ate, and in the basement she selected the Z-shaped grate shaker beside the furnace; went back to the bedroom.

The chest, she found, was heavy. But she pulled it away from the wall, catching the vase of flowers as it fell. She put the flowers on a dressing table. Her thin arms were hardened by tennis on English lawns. 

Then there was a crash. The chest sprawled heels up. 

The light was fading outside. In the bedroom it was murky. She lighted a wall bracket.

She swung the grate shaker on the underneath side of the chest. A thin board grunted, she swung three times more. The board broke and she tore it away. She smashed the left side of the drawer bottom.

Her searching hand dragged out a pair of Murray Bay blankets, an old Chinese priest's robe, cashmere and silk dress materials. She threw them on the floor and attacked the bottom of the next drawer with the Z-shaped shaker.

Out of this second drawer came rubber-banded cancelled checks, old insurance policies, old letters, memo books in a fanciful script, photographs-several showing Leslie Cobb as a child-faded magazine clippings, mostly poetry. But at the left-almost halfway the depth of the drawer-she encountered a metal box. It was fastened by rivets to the front and side of the drawer, she found, after ripping away the entire drawer bottom. 

She swung the iron shaker against the underside of the box-made only dents. She swung again. 

The sweat was on her upper lip and forehead, it got into her eyes. She clawed at the metal box, broke a fingernail, whimpered. She picked up the shaker and flailed the width of the metal box, striking where the back joined it. "Open up, open up, open up, open up."

At last she stopped, she sat on her heels staring at the wreckage-but the next moment she was brushing the sweat from her eyes. The narrow back of the box had separated in one corner. 

Four blows with the shaker. A gap of one inch. She beat the metal further back until she could shove the shaker handle into the opening, throw her body on it. 

The whole end came away.

She pulled out a bundle of papers and tore her knuckles against the broken rivets. She carried the bundle over to the wall light, shuffled through documents. 

. . . Oh! . . ."

She leaned weakly against the wall and looked as if she wanted to cry in a flood; but she could not. She could merely look at a folded paper, heavy, with a thin double red line at the left-the stationery lawyers like to use.

AGREEMENT 

Sept. 27, 1928

I, Leslie Phelps Varden, because of certain facts in the possession of my aunt, Miss Sophia Pollard, and for reasons the said Sophia Pollard and I mutually concede are sufficient and urgent in view of my conduct, which threatens to bring disgrace to a family name both old and honorable, and being of sound mind and acting of my own free will, do hereby agree: (1) to leave the boundaries of the United States of 

America within 14 days of this date; and (2) not to return; provided (3) I accept an annuity of $25,000 from the said Sophia Pollard or her legal representatives (4) to be given me so long as I live up to this agreement. 

(signed) LESLIE P. Varden WITNESSES: 

L. Plunkett 

T. R. Whittle Sr.

Leslie Cobb picked up her bag on the bed, drew out a gold-and-enamel cigarette lighter.... It did not light.

After the fourth try she threw the thing back into her bag, went into a bathroom to the left of the four-poster bed, tore the agreement into small squares, threw them in the toilet, which she flushed twice. "Well, that's that!"

Back in the bedroom she took a pack of cigarettes from her purse; again thumbed the lighter. It worked this time.

A look at her watch seemed to frighten her. She

hurried to restore the contents of the chest, replace the splintered drawer bottoms. She half-squatted for the struggle, and then the chest crashed on its side. She did not let it rest. She pushed it back on its legs, shoved it against the wall and returned the vase of asters. 

She looked around, corrected with her heel the wrinkles in an Aubusson rug, snapped out the light.

Downstairs, in the pantry, the dachshund, somewhat pot-bellied, was cleaning its mouth with its tongue. 

Leslie Cobb shoved the dish under the refrigerator. "Good-by, Otto, old gal.”

The dog's toenails scratched the floor. It didn't want to be pushed into the living room. Outside.

It was lighter than she'd realized. 

She walked around a gladiolus bed toward the roadway, and stopped. Behind her was the Sound. She turned toward the water, nostrils dilating to the sea breeze, she walked under thick-waisted oaks to a small white shed-a summer pavilion facing a cement walk above the sea wall.

Some men were fishing, away off yonder. The water looked like carelessly wrinkled cellophane. On its sea side the little pavilion had a porch with lean-to roof held by three white posts. Under this lean-to Leslie Cobb found a wicker couch, wicker table and two chairs like straw hoopskirts. .

Still reflective, still casual, she turned from the water and cut across some grass at the right of the little house. 

She was passing a bed of President Hoover dahlias just around the south corner from the pavilion-she leaned down to smell the young buds, stared dreamily.

.... Suddenly her eyes seemed to focus. The short-hewn grass was scuffed. Puzzled, she saw that across some of the dahlias' leaves,-at a corner of the bed some seven or eight feet from the pavilion,-lurked some spots, a splash of them, some as large as a half-dollar, some the size of a dime or less. 

Leslie Cobb looked up, looked to right and left. She shrugged, started on-moved exactly five steps and stopped. A small metal object in the grass held her. 

She touched it with her toe.

Bending over, she picked it up, rubbed its partly sooty surface with finger and thumb; turned it on end and read. Frowning, she walked back to the President 

Hoover dahlias, selected several leaves, wrapped them in her handkerchief and thrust the bundle in her bag. 

Color had left her face until her dark tropical tan was unwholesomely yellow.

The metal object she dropped in the coin purse of her bag; it clinked dully against some silver. It was the brass carcass of a cartridge-.32 Colt automatic. 

The next moment her long racehorse legs were covering the open stretch of sand-she hurried through gardens, past flowerhouse, garage, Superintendent's cottage.

The iron door thumped shut. She returned to the car. "Drive back," she said. "I want to go to Kingree & 

Merrick's real-estate office on Main Street. I'll tell you, when you reach the Square."

He turned the car, picked up speed. They were a quarter-mile south when an Auburn with sporty grey top approached. 

Just before the Auburn reached them, Leslie Cobb saw it, stared as if recognizing the driver. She threw herself down-crouched behind the driver's compartment.

CHAPTER III -I 

Thursday A. M., June 4th 

TAMPING her cigarette Leslie Cobb picked up the hand telephone. "Darien 81833-ring 2..." The quarreling of the new Swedish houseman and his wife drifted up from the kitchen. "Hello, Miss Plunkett? Mrs. Cobb. 

Feel more rested this morning? I'm so glad. . . . Yes, after a fashion; but when your home's been rented for four years it seems hopeless of ever straightening out again. Jack? I called the hospital this morning. 

Resting comfortably, they said. . . . Valery has been asking to see you. Yes, I may bring her over. 'Bye-bye."

She put the phone back with a click, lifted it, called a New York number: PEnnsylvania 6-2214. She spoke close to the mouthpiece. "Hello... this is Mrs. Averhill of the New Jersey 

Flower Society. May I have the report now on thatyes, that's right." .. 

Waiting, facing a window, her eyes aimlessly following a chipmunk that crouched along the stone wall behind the barn-Leslie Cobb suddenly gripped the telephone. "It was blood?" She had difficulty swallowing. 

Beastly abrupt, that chemist. "Could you I wonder if you-was it human blood?... It was? Thank you very much."

From her twelve-acre estate on Newfield road out north 34

from Stamford she could see, to the east, over the trees and hills, Long Island Sound.... Somewhere over there, in the dull mirror of water, Sophia Pollard's Shorecrest dozed in the sun. 

The flesh puckered between Leslie Cobb's eyes. She pushed back her hair at the left temple where it fell away to her ear, lighted a cigarette, stood up.

She put out the cigarette, walked over to a mirror hanging on a green cord, looked at herself. After a moment she found herself at the telephone stand, lighting another cigarette.

She put out the cigarette, broke it between her fingers, slowly rubbed the tobacco crumbs.

She walked up and down the library, which was on the second floor and extended the width of the farmhouse. It had studio windows up to the ceiling. . . . On the fourth turn she stopped, went to the bathroom and spat into the washbowl. It was blood. She rinsed her mouth.

On the way downstairs she drew on a white jacket over her white shantung sports dress. 

Miss Blythe, the governess, sat under an apple tree near the old well with its high pole and wooden keg. 

There was a doll and a book in her lap. Valery leaned over her back, arms around her neck. 

Leslie Cobb said: "Will you put on Valery's coat and hat. And tell What's-Her-Name to put another place for luncheon." "Yes, Mrs. Cobb. I say, Mrs. Cobb, this sand-box is quite impossible." Miss Blythe added, “Cats.” "Do tell the gardener, I'm in a hurry." And Leslie

Cobb stepped into a new Ford cabriolet. It had a black body and sand-colored cloth top. She had trouble start-

ing the motor. Miss Blythe lifted in the little girl. Blond curls fumed up from a tweed beret. "Hurry, child.” 

Leslie Cobb cut into a chilled artichoke heart and spoke across the table to Miss Blythe. "You're right. It is warm." "Like the sultriness at Le Touquet. Remember?” 

Miss Blythe then remarked to Miss Plunkett: "Mrs. Cobb played golf so I took the jouncing little tram to Etaples. 

It gave me quite a turn. My brother was in hospital there and the Jerries flew over with bombs. A rum un, Jerry was. Killed a thousand wounded Tommies lying in their beds in the hospital huts. Dropped one bomb right in the milit'ry cemetery. That was in May, '18." "It is warm," Leslie Cobb said steadily to Miss 

Blythe. "And that's why I was wondering why Aunt

Sophia happened to be in town. I recall she always came out here to her Stamford place very early. She used to come out the middle of April, invariably- 99 

The sun at this moment caught Miss Plunkett's glasses.

She cut into her sturgeon in aspic. Her teeth were loose today so she ate rapidly. Leslie Cobb got up and eased the Venetian blinds. "English, aren't you?" Miss Plunkett asked. "Yes," Miss Blythe smiled. She shifted some sturgeon with her knife to the back of her fork, and ate. 

Leslie Cobb addressed Miss Blythe: "I wonder how it happened that Aunt was in town so late?" 

Old Miss Plunkett's mouth and lips moved irrationally. 

Perhaps it was her teeth; they wouldn't stay up. Anyway, she said: "She was only in town that night." "Oh, I see," Leslie Cobb turned to her. "Supposed they told you," Miss Plunkett remarked.

She kept her attention on her plate. "Miss Pollard was getting very queer, Mrs. Cobb. Her ways were very queer for some time. . . . Sunday night, it was some time after midnight, she called me, insisted we go in to New 

York. Said she didn't feel well. 'Lucy take me to the

Fifth Avenue house,' she said to me. She said she wanted to have Dr. Spoors see her early next morning. And I said, 'Why can't I telephone him to come out here?' 

Come that same night, that is." "I see." “But no, she wanted to go in to New York. She wouldn't let me call Johnny Smith." Miss Plunkett was breaking a hot biscuit. "He's our chauffeur. I don't think he was with us when you were here before?" 

Leslie Cobb sipped iced tea. "I really don't believe so." "No," Miss Plunkett said quickly-"nothing would do but that I drive her in." She said to Miss Blythe, who was helping Valery with her custard: "I had to do everything for her." "Fancy that." 

Leslie Cobb said: "I remember Miss Plunkett was always having rows with those motorcycle police. She's a demon driver." "Fancy that," said Miss Blythe. 

Soberly, to the Englishwoman, Leslie Cobb remarked: "I'm glad Aunt wasn't ill long. It's so sad when one suffers a long time.” 

Miss Plunkett watched the Swede in the white jacket as he served blueberries. Presently she said: "Women were born to suffer." 

Leslie Cobb said to Miss Blythe: "She was in her eighties but she was in excellent health for a woman of her age. 

---

"Fancy that," said Miss Blythe. 

Miss Plunkett spoke sharply. "Excuse me! How do you know she was in excellent health? Weren't you over there for four years?" 

Leslie Cobb's eyes-so startling, so inarticulate, within her lashes and darkened complexion-turned to the old woman. "Why? Wasn't she really?"

Miss Plunkett was shaken with emotion. "She was a different woman from what you know. I 'tended her for thirty-seven years-knew her better than her own mother. She changed." "Sundquist," Leslie Cobb said, "will you bring some cigarettes, please." Lazily she asked: "How do you mean-changed?" 

The old woman's lips were unsteady. Her eyes were dour and secretive. "She was a changed woman," she said.

Valery was holding her custard spoon toward the screen door. Leslie Cobb said to Miss Blythe, "What should we do with young women who point with their spoons ?" "Bees," said Valery. "They're bumble bees. They sting you if you don't look smart." 

Leslie Cobb turned to Miss Plunkett. "I'm sorry

I..." Miss Plunkett's teeth were giving trouble again. "I said" the fork in her hand underscored the "said"- "they put the devil in her. It may be terrible of me to say it but I do say it. They deviled her into” she peered at Leslie Cobb "-when you left—”

Leslie Cobb took a fleck of tobacco off her tongue. "I've forgotten all about it."

Miss Plunkett seemed to rise in her chair. "But they didn't, not for a minute. They didn't let her forget it;

they harped at her . . . bossed her. Especially that de, testable Whittle. He knows what I think of him. . . .

Why, she used to care more about you, Leslie Cobb, than all of them in a knitting bag, and if—" "Pardon me," Leslie Cobb said. “I believe Valery is ready for her nap, Miss Blythe?" After the two left she said: “Why? Wasn't she happy?" 

The spinster said: "You might think so" and added, "I would not." "What do you mean by that?" "Mrs. Cobb, your Aunt was brought up a decent Christian after the good New England way. She lived a 

Christian woman-always. Some might've said she was a trifle firm in her ways—" "Oh, no; you exaggerate." "-But she lived Holy Scriptures, no one will deny that, Mrs. Cobb. She had great wealth, yes. It meant responsibility to your Aunt Sophia, and you'll never know how many secret kindnesses- "I'm sure she did." 

Miss Plunkett's face was ugly with stress as she said: "You might as well know it now." 

Leslie Cobb blew smoke upon the cigarette in her hand and the ash blazed red. "Know what?" "About the drinking. They encouraged her, Mrs. Cobb, three years ago, about-'twas after you left to drink those poisons." "What in God's name are you raving about?" "Humph!" Miss Plunkett gazed over her eyeglasses. "Well?... I asked you." "Liquor's poison, ain't it? Whiskey's poison, ain't it?

Champagne's poison and wine. 'Least, I was always

taught so.... Look not thou upon the wine when it is red... at the last it biteth like a serpent!" 

Her voice cracked in mockery. 66 "Purely medicinal.' . . . 'Harmless stimulant, do her good.' Oh, yes; oh my, yes. So they said. And that smart-Alec Spoors jabbering away exactly like they wanted him to. The great New York doctor, oh, my. 

Forcing filthy poisons down that dear good woman's throat, who'd never touched but wine at the Lord's

Supper- 

Her anger flared again. "Why, she-she had to have champagne every night, it got so. That's as true as I'm sitting here!" "You are right," Leslie Cobb said; "she changed. See here, did she have champagne the other night?" "What other night?" "You know the night I mean-Sunday, night she died."

Miss Plunkett didn't say anything. She ground on her teeth, her lips moving, but she didn't say anything. "Did she?" 

No answer. "I'm glad I am not in your shoes." 

Miss Plunkett glared. "What do you mean?" "Don't you see?... You were the only one with Sophia 

Pollard when she died. And she'd been drinking heavily and for some reason-or so you say-she insists around midnight on leaving her house, climbing into a car, driving thirty-five miles-alone with you- 99 

The color seeped out of Miss Plunkett's old face. "Alone with me? Quite right! I am not afraid of that. I can face my Maker with a clean conscience and a pure heart, Mrs. Cobb. At least, I did more for her

than anybody else than her dancing, drinking family." 

She nodded her head. "I stuck by her to the end.”

Miss Plunkett wouldn't answer another question. She held her agitated lips in place, she tapped the unused dessert spoon with her fingers. The best thing, she said finally, was for her to go home . . . “Only place I've known as home for thirty-seven years. But the Lord only can say how long that'll be." 

So Leslie Cobb telephoned the Sophia Pollard place for one of the cars. Johnny Smith, a watery-eyed, fustylooking old fellow with bow legs, came and took Miss 

Plunkett away. 

Thursday P. M., June 4th 

At 2:30 Ann Phelps called. She said her son Jack was out of danger but she feared his face would be scarred. She said the family was going to meet with the lawyers informally on John's boat, have a buffet supper aboard and cruise about the Sound for the evening. "You're right," Leslie Cobb said. "There's been at least a dozen here. I'm supposed to rattle off interviews on 'How It Feels to Be a Millionaire Heiress' but I'm in no rags-to-riches mood.... Yes, I'll be down." 

At 5:10 the black-hulled Tulsa II, a 68-foot, twin-screw commuter-cruiser with two 300 h.p. gas engines, knifed a breeze unlike the sultriness on shore, thrummed along the Sound near the steamer lane off Eaton's Point and 

Norwalk. Water was green, deep and calm.

Margery Phelps came out of the lounge room. She sat beside Leslie Cobb in a leather chair on the after-deck. "As soon as this"-a pretty sigh-"is over, I want Neville to get a yacht too. He's quite worn out. We'll

cruise up north, first. I feel so dowdy. Did you bring back many new clothes? I'm quite in rags. It's a nuisance, being in mourning all winter." "Isn't it?" · · "I want so to give a really squiffy ball at Sherry's, or p'r'aps Pierre's, it's quite smart there now, our friends have been so lovely about everything . I feel so sad today, I've been thinking about dear Mother, she always said: 'After all, my dear, we are descendants of one of the oldest and most respected New York families; we can't ever forget that and it's no good letting down the bars.' Mother simply couldn't endure claptrap. Oh, Leslie, I wish we were English!" 

Leslie Cobb asked, "Why do you?" and watched two racing outboards scribble white lines out from Compo beach. She couldn't see the pilots for the plumes they threw up. “Oh, Leslie, they do preserve the social graces. Isn't that rather lovely in this age when everybody's letting down her hair and screeching 'Whoops'? The British are so inflexible. You must admit that." "Broke-but proud." 

Margery felt she ought to smile. "Why, I thought they were your best friends. After all, you married a 

Lord's sonson" "He was broke too." "Leslie, you're impossible. How long do you think we ought to stay in mourning? I was saving to Tatiana just this morning- 99 "Aunt sort of dampened your social career, didn't she?" "Between you and me-but don't tell Neville I said it-it's been perfectly ghastly. Why, she raised hell every

time we took the hunts crowd on a binge, or entertained in New York. She had it in for me." "She did, really?"

Margery brushed the hair stuttering across her face. "She had the nerve to say to me, she actually did: 'Your greed for social power will ruin Neville. He hasn't a cent and won't have until I die. Why truck with Society emptyheads-your grandfather was a miller.' Oh 

Leslie! Now I ask you" 

Leslie Cobb saw Ricks Whittle approach. "As a matter of fact," she rose and watched the water rushing astern, "you could have killed her with your own hands. Couldn't you, Margery?" 

Margery's little mouth curled in malice. "You're so hard-boiled. You remind me of that actress I met at the 

Colony Club." "Hullo, Ricks. Margery thinks I'm hard-boiled." 

Whittle leaned with one hand on the rail. "That so? 

Then there's no hope for me?" "Oh, I didn't say that," Margery said; "but Leslie-"

Eleanor Duane was calling from the deckhouse. "Food," she said. "Ann wants to know if the boy should bring it out here on the back porch, or will everybody meander into the lower depths?" "Oh," Margery looked at the other two, "I think it's much too windy on deck. Come on!" "Wait a minute, Ricks."

Leslie Cobb's mouth was sullen. The cruiser lurched in a wave. She caught an awning support. Whittle grabbed her other arm and when she lowered it he let his hand stop over hers. "Now what?" "See here. Why did you feed her liquor?"

"Feed who liquor?" "I never knew you to be stupid." "Oh, come, Leslie! I didn't 'feed' her liquor. Dr.

Spoors prescribed it, said it was a tonic. What's all the fuss? I didn't corrupt the old lady." 

Twilight darkened her face, brought out the silver contrast of her eyes, shamelessly revealed something of the secret inner struggle she waged with her nature. "Suppose that somebody accused you," she said, "of making a drunkard of her-for ulterior purposes. Somebody might believe you have profited handsomely at her death. And you and your father alone know the contents of her will. Somebody might even say that you- 

"Who is this 'somebody'? Leslie, what kind of a dog d'you think me? I've always had unalterable faith in you; gosh, I think you're as fine as they make them." "Not falling in love, Ricks?" "Leslie, don't do that." "Don't do what?" "Don't snicker." "All right, I won't snicker if you don't want me to." 

Suddenly she said: “As for motive, there's your brother, Ricks." She saw his mouth harden. "Why did Ted take his life?" 

His face went dead white. "We won't talk about that now."

He squinted into the sun-purple and grey horizontal stripes like a lustrous awning behind the Southport shoreline. "Why not?" "If I didn't have faith in you"-with an effort he

turned to her "I'd ask: Exactly where were you last 

Sunday night?" "Why ask, if you have?" "Well, you weren't aboard the Maury." 

She was richly amused. "That's faith," she said.. "So you've been checking up on me, Ricks? Maybe you're worried-are you?—whether I killed Aunt Sophia out of my lusty hate for her, as, exhilarated by your 

Pol Roger tonic, she refused- 

Leslie Cobb turned her head. John Phelps stood in the doorway of the deckhouse. "You two coming in?” 

She looked at Ricks. He said, "Coming, John."

Inside, a steward in a white monkey jacket was shaking cocktails. The elder Whittle, who had large yellow front teeth, was telling Eleanor Duane and Ann Phelps of the old days when gentlemen were sailors and the good Sound air wasn't polluted with gasoline fumes and outboard hubbub. 

Ann called, "Isn't it rather cold on deck? I think it's chilly." She said to the boy, "Please fix the fireplace, Edward." He snapped the switch on some imitation logs. "It'll warm up in a minute," Ann said. "What'll you have, Mr. Whittle? Aren't you starved?" "This gives you an appetite," Ricks said. "It's a lovely table," Leslie Cobb said, covertly watching him. "I shan't do a thing to the hors-d'oeuvres.” 

Margery Phelps was taking more cold lobster. "But that is smörgåsbord, my dear. What must your Continental feasting have been?" "Leslie's chief diet has been li-on steaks in the Transvaal," said Mrs. Duane. "Oh, anyway-one of those nigger jungles."

"Wrong," said Neville. "Filet of zebra." He handed

Leslie Cobb a dry Martini from a group on a platter. 

At 7:38 the man in the Tulsa II's wheelhouse sighted

New Haven. The sea was calm. The Tulsa II overhauled a large schooner broad on the starboard bow. The schooner had the right of way. The quartermaster swung his wheel. In ten minutes the Tulsa II passed astern.

In the lounge the roll of water against the Tulsa II's side caused John Phelps to spill some of his drink. "You'd think we were going to a fire," he said, sitting in the corner beside Leslie Cobb, who was studying over the library shelf. The others were somewhat apart from them. "I think it's a splendid boat,” she answered. "Think so? I'm going to have it overhauled. I've used it quite a bit, entertaining oil men. They play rough." 

She said: "Didn't Aunt kick about your having such an expensive plaything for the visiting wild well tamers?" 

John said, “H'm!" She said: "Well, you can afford to buy a new one now, if you care to." She turned the leaves of a book. "John, I feel so badly about Jack." 

His eyes narrowed. To her surprise he said: "Everybody insists it was that damned flashlight powder, but 

I don't." She asked a question, had to repeat it. All he would say was, "Don't know yet, myself." Almost so she had to strain to hear him he said: "You and young 

Whittle talking about Aunt?"

She slouched in her chair, book on her knee. Without looking up she said, “Merely gossiping, John. Why?” 

His weighty head was close. "Keeping something from me, Leslie?"

"Um, d'know. 99 "Let's have it. They can't hear what we're talking about. What is it?" "It was nothing. We-rather, I was just romancing. 

About Aunt. Just my crazy imagination, you know.

It's not worth talking about-women's imaginings." 

She looked swiftly at him. He was silent. The afternoon's sun still lay red on his large face. The flesh was knotted between his eyes. "Tell me, anyway." "If you won't laugh?"-she began. "Well, suppose that Aunt died suddenly-I mean, even more suddenly than we believe. (You must think me quite foolish?) 

Well, just suppose that." "Quite suddenly?" "Yes." "You mean, not a natural death?” "Yes, John.' 99 

He looked at the others, at her. "What makes you think that? Surely you don't start talking such things without suspicion." He muttered something about preposterous. "John, don't bulldoze me." "I want you to tell me. Don't be idiotic." "Well, am I idiotic' if I say-in utmost confidence? you swear it?—that Aunt was shot? And on the grounds of her shore place? Not in town, I mean. Is that 'idiotic' ?" "I don't know. What've you got to go by?" 

She opened the book again. "Oh, womanly intuition. 

That's as good as any

At 9:38, off Saybrook, with the lighthouses off the port bow, the Tulsa II, per sailing orders, turned for the return to Stamford. Leslie Cobb, Ricks Whittle and Neville Phelps leaned on the after-deck rail. The others preferred the warmth below. "Ever been to Saybrook? Don't!" Neville said. "Yes, up there, where those lights are-never saw so many old dames and codgers." "It was the dames soured you," said Leslie Cobb.

Eleanor Duane, with a coat over her shoulders, came up. "I must be losing my appeal," she drawled. "Nobody wants to taalk to me." 

Whittle said, "Oh, come now, Eleanor." 

She took his arm. "All right, then. Let's walk up there." He didn't seem glad about going. 

After a moment Leslie Cobb said to Neville: "Who's the latest? Any girl I used to know? 'Course, I've been away a long time." 

His grin warmed one. "I'm real stuck this time,” he said. He snapped his fingers, like a dice player. "But you don't know her." 

She put her hand on his arm. "Don't you think the 

Countess Pflomm is a little over your head, Neville?”

He looked at her insolently. "After all, Neville, she's no silly debby, or Broadway girl."

His grasp on her arm hurt. But she swung around.

He faced her. "You think you're awfully goddamned clever. Don't you?" "Why, no, Neville; I've made some very foolish mistakes in my day." "You're making one now-understand?" "But I was only spoofing-" He was lighting a cigarette shakily, his hands cupped against the wind. “If you feared I'd trot to Margery-" she added. "Only . with the Countess Pflomm as your house guest, I couldn't help wondering when trouble'd begin." "Know her?" "Casually. By the way, Neville, how did Aunt take to her?"

He was quite shaken with anger now. "You know so much, you ought to know that too."

She leaned back against the rail, the water stewing and hissing close below her. "I'll bet she took the little Russian to her more or less wilted bosom." She was laughing quite recklessly. "And the Countess-frail, Stalinbeaten moth-was so hurt, and you were so enamoured . . that you two decided to ditch the works? Hey, Neville?" Then her voice dropped in mocking tragedy. "But one can see how it is! Poor Countess, poor Neville.

If they did that-Aunt would stick to her threat-she'd cut him off. And she was the kind of Aunt who meant what she said. Wasn't she, Neville?"

He struck at her with the back of his hand. She managed to move but his fingers touched her mouth. "You know too much for your own good," he said, and left her. "Oh, dear," she lamented, "I've made myself quite unpleasant to the whole ship's company. And it's all very little worth-while."

She turned and leaned on the rail. The rail was really too low here. It would be dangerous with children aboard. The water looked so cool, so buoyant, thick like flowing oil. As a girl she used to sail on this water with 

Neville and Ricks and Ten Varden. They called their sloop The Houn' Dog because it seemed always ready to lie down.

Now, tonight, she was twenty-eight and she'd been almost everywhere and had had two husbands and there didn't seem anywhere further to go. . . . . . She could do like that French tennis player Alain Gerbault and take a little boat and sail for years to out-of-the-way places. 

But then there was Valery, poor child. 

At 10:18, off Sachem Head, the Tulsa II was making twenty miles, and the radio in the lounge beat a rhythm for the shuffle of the engines below. Leslie Cobb, at the rail, could hear now and then the voices of the others in the deckhouse.

Suddenly she felt pain in the middle of her back, between her shoulder blades-a pain from a hard blow, and she said "Oh-h-" weakly; but that was all. The next moment she fell face down, and struck the foaming water on her stomach.

The blow in the back and the hard fall wracked her lungs, paralyzed her.

She sank heavily, dragged by her tweed coat, the whip of the twin propeller blades. It seemed a long time. . . She tried to swim. But her arms were stubborn.

The water was over her head and she wanted very much to find the surface, for she was choking. 

But there did not seem to be any surface; it was all dead weight crushing her down, and her lungs hurt, and then she couldn't keep her mouth closed any longer,

and the water tasted salty. She was choking sick on the salty water.

She fought away the salty water with her hands, and then her feet took up the fight and she wanted badly to scream very loudly-she did scream, and gulped salt water.

She wanted to summon Ten Varden, he had always helped her, he would laugh in that grand way he had and she'd feel his big arms. "Ten, oh Ten!" But it was quite silly calling Ten, wasn't it? He was dead. Dead? 

Dead! And then she got rid of the coat, her shoes. But she could not stand any more. She was tired. She relaxed horribly. • · · SO After a while the night air brushed her face, and she opened her mouth and breathed; it was very hard, but she started swimming. Then the water was out of her mouth. And out of her eyes too, but she was so tired she could not keep going much longer. "For God's sake"

The Tulsa II, speed unchecked, was far ahead; in a moment it rushed below Leslie Cobb's horizon.

CHAPTER IV  MISS was ISS BETTY SMITH of the Bridgeport Smiths was reluctant. She denied with some indignation that she was afraid. "It's cold, that's why," she said. "Well, then, take a drink," said Mr. Marty Witkowski. "Go on, Betty, take your dress off. Look at Stella." 

Miss Stella Fink giggled. Miss Fink had a sports jacket over her shoulders. She sat on a leather cushion in Mr. Witkowski's motorboat. 

Mr. Red Toumey now said, "I'll show you how to do it." He grasped Miss Betty Smith's dress. Miss Smith said, "Behave, Red." Mr. Toumey pulled the dress over her head and Miss Smith in a muffled voice said: "Well, wait a minute, you dumbbell; I have to unbutton them sleeves." 

The motorboat, steered by Mr. Witkowski, proceeded past Pine Orchard at six miles an hour. 

Presently Miss Fink said: "Marty, why don't you stop here? It looks perfectly grand here. I love to swim in deep water, don't you, Marty?" 

Mr. Witkowski said, "Well, I need a drink first." He reached back, took the bottle from Mr. Toumey. The boat engine was in good shape, Mr. Witkowski, an assistant foreman in a Bridgeport brass mill, had seen to that.

But it had a noisy exhaust and it shook the boat quite a 52

bit. They had passed a large black-hulled cruiser a few miles back and Miss Smith had cried: "Oh, boy, I could go for the fella who's got that." 

Mr. Toumey had said: "Yes? I bet he ain't on a threeday week, like we birds. You don't have to stay here, goom-bye." 

This had made Miss Smith laugh, because look weren't they out in Long Island Sound and how could she leave at that moment? A few minutes later she had removed 

Mr. Toumey's hand from her waist saying, "Give me room to grow in, big boy." 

But now everything was K. O. As Miss Fink said, "How can you swim if you are held in by bathing suits like baby clothes? Oh, boy. This is swell."

Mr. Witkowski now cut the switch, guiding the boat into the wind. "Oh, boy," Miss Fink said. She bent over the pipe rail and Mr. Witkowski playfully struck her shoulder. "It's cold, I think it is cold," Miss Smith said.

Mr. Toumey had his arm over her shoulder. "It's good when you get in there," he said. "Wait till you get in there." 

Miss Smith said, "Listen! Oh, shut up, Stella!"

The engine was dead now. Suddenly Mr. Witkowski jumped overboard. He came swimming back, stroking with his right arm only and holding a woman's head above water with his left. "Jeez, Red, grab her."

Mr. Toumey leaned over and caught the woman's dress in the back. Miss Fink got hold of an arm. "Take it easy, Stella!" warned Mr. Witkowski from the water. "If you ship water you got to bail it out, I won't help you."

Friday A. M., June 5th

It was about 8:30 and dusk when she turned her car off 

Willowbrook Avenue by the shore. The gates were open and she drove into Shorecrest. "Evening, Mrs. Cobb."

It was Johnny Smith. His puttees made him look even more bow-legged than he was. She pushed forward on both pedals, the car stopped. "Good evening." In the half-light under the trees her dark complexion made her silvered eyes opaque and passionate. "Have you a match-er-"

"Everybody calls me Johnny. I was an oysterman yonder on the Sound and so was my papa. Miss Pollard, she always called me Johnny." He cupped his hands for the light. "Thank you-Johnny. I don't believe you were here when I went abroad?" "I would of remembered if you was."

He scraped the gravel with his toe. Twenty-five feet beyond, upstairs over the garage in the servants' quarters,

Rudy Vallee sang through his nose. Leslie Cobb beckoned with a movement of her head and Johnny came up to the running board. "Weren't you jealous of Miss Plunkett, Johnny? I hear Aunt Sophia preferred to have her drive her around, instead of you." 

His old face wrinkled in a grin. "No'm, I wasn't jealous. I drove Miss Pollard for three year, four months, and I drove the late Mr. Bedford Dean for two; I can always get a driving job to do." "I'll wager you can! But see here, you didn't drive 

Aunt last week-night she went to town?" "That time I did not and I just don't know how that come about yet. But I sort of snoozed." "Well, how did you find out Miss Plunkett had driven her in to New York?" "Well," he said, "I heard somebody starting up the motor. That was about 3 o'clock. 'Course, I recognized it was the old Loco. I looked out the window-I got that room on the right upstairs-and happened to see

Miss Plunkett getting in. She turned the Loco and drove down there to the house. That sort of surprised me, as Miss Plunkett never allowed cars to be drove up to the house before. So I went back to bed." "Was it Miss Plunkett's custom to drive Aunt around late at night?" "It was and it wasn't. Sometimes I did. 'Fact, I generally did. She had her own ways." "Good night, Johnny."

She drove across the forbidden sandspit to the house on the water. The house was lighted on both first and second floors. Her knees sagged as she stepped out of the car. She still was pretty weak, weaker than she'd imagined.

After a moment she said, "I don't know, I'm the cook." She had stolid blue eyes. “Maybe she is over there," and she moved a thick bare arm. "Over where?" "Maybe over by summer house." 

Leslie Cobb found the little sea pavilion dark under its lean-to roof. . . . "Hello? You there, Miss Plunkett?"

She saw the old woman staring out across the water.

Ahead of them, across the cement promenade, the tide hissed wet against the sea wall, the water smelled like cold clambroth. Far ahead, between them and the black soft-lead penciling of Long Island, the stammer of motorcraft made Leslie Cobb all the more conscious of the strange, morbid atmosphere in the summer place. Under this sad, electric spell she went on: "You are, I know, a religious woman. A very religious woman." Miss

Plunkett gave no notice of hearing and Leslie Cobb remarked: "But you have no monopoly on the Scriptures, my dear.

You used to find me in church on many a Sunday. And my darkest moments-and I have known dark moments—I've found guidance and help in that Book as I feel in you do." 

That hiss-slap rhythm of the water just beyond their feet! . . . And Leslie Cobb murmuring phrases-with an indolent perversity-phrases newly committed to memory: "None calleth for justice, nor any pleadeth for truth: they trust in vanity-" But the old woman gave no sign she heard and Leslie Cobb said quietly: "For your hands are defiled with blood, and your fingers with iniquity. Remember how that goes, Miss Plunkett? 

About how your tongue hath muttered perverseness .?" Leslie Cobb saw her mouth tremble in profile, she was watching her and she saw her mouth tremble. She got no further for Miss Plunkett suddenly was on her feet crying "Oh, do hold your tongue," and her voice was like a man's, high-cracked, flat; and she sought to put her hand on Leslie Cobb's mouth and as they struggled Miss Plunkett in a fiercely energetic way said: "Arise! Shine! For thy light is come . . . and the glory of the Lord, oh, the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee"; and she fell back in her chair, and bent down into her hands that were so bony, so old. 

Leslie Cobb threw her cold cigarette on the walk, and waited, and the old woman said through her fingers, "Be merciful unto me, O God-I am old and greyheaded, O God, forsake me not." Now she seemed to rock back and forth, she talked with difficulty, as if chilled by some terror; there was a pause. "Save me, O

God," she said, simply. "The waters are come into my soul, I am weary of my crying."

After a minute Leslie Cobb seized the old woman's arm to silence her and very quietly she said: "Now I want the truth. Did you hear anything around herethat night?"

Plunkett say something, she demanded: "What was that?" "I said "Yes." "
