I loved her "Answer me."

The old woman turned to her, chin up, voice metallic. "I will. I've got to. My answer is-I don't know." "Don't know? Of course you do." "I found her dead-right there on that walk, already a dear little angel of God in Heaven,-right out there on that walk almost at your feet and as God is my witness- 

"Then why did you keep it a secret? Why did you tell everyone she became ill and you drove her at her request to New York at 3 or 4 in the morning-that she died peacefully in her bed? Why did you lie?" 

Miss Plunkett was scornful. "Why?" Because there was a hole in the side of her head, here"-she touched her right temple somewhat back from the hairline. "And another hole on the other side, in the back. 

That's why, Mrs. Cobb! It was no way for Sophia Pollard to die and I wasn't going to have anybody know it-not disgrace like that." 

For the first time, Miss Plunkett cried. But the tears seemed to dry as fast as they fell in her throat. "It—it's been so such a terrible secret to keep. Knowing all that. Oh! I have asked my God for counsel, for guidance. I was praying when you came." "The old New England conscience"

"My conscience! My conscience!" Miss Plunkett turned on her. "I knew sometime I had to tell somebody," and added, "besides God." "Supposed He knew already.” 

Miss Plunkett became very upset. "I don't think that 

I understand you, Mrs. Cobb." "Did you do it?" 

Miss Plunkett jumped to her feet. "I do not have to be talked to that way; I won't stand for it." "Don't go!" Leslie Cobb put an arm around the gaunt unloved body. "Come back. Tell me what happened. 

Forgive me. I'm not myself." 

Sophia Pollard in her eighty-fourth year-according to her secretary-preferred to spend her summer evenings, when the weather was agreeable, in this pavilion overlooking Long Island Sound, and also on the cemented walk. "They all knew it," Miss Plunkett said at one point. "But they left it up to me to do the 'tending of her. 

About 11 o'clock each night-after those servants'd gone to their quarters up there at the garage; no, she'd have only me stay with her at the house—she'd go up to her room. She'd take off all her clothes, every stitch, her dress, underthings, corsets, stockings, shoes. She'd put on an old terry-cloth bathrobe. It wasn't at all clean but she had to have just that one. A body couldn't talk her out of it. Just that bathrobe and some red silk stockings and red satin evening shoes she had twenty-five years."

Miss Plunkett looked questioning at Leslie Cobb, who said, "Um, yes. Go on." "So she'd send me to the kitchen. I'd fetch a bucket with the champagne and ice."

"Yes-and then?" "Then I'd go out with her-come here. And she'd sit here by this table, she'd drink! Many a night she'd send me back for some of those wretched French poisons first, before she'd start in on the champagne. Cointreau, or chartreuse. She'd drink a good bit of that first. 

Then the champagne. And she'd scold at me: 'Where are the sandwiches, you stupid thing? Do you want to spoil the ball?'” "Spoil the what?" "She insisted night after night it was a fancy dress ball or a Governor's affair, and she would laugh something terrible, like a silly young girl. You know. And then I'd bring some caviar sandwiches perhaps. Or sturgeon and Swiss cheese, or anchovy. And she'd say, 'Oh, Mr. Geoffrey' or 'Major Desmond'-oh," said Miss 

Plunkett wryly, "any saucy name came in her head. 

She'd say: 'Oh, Mr. Geoffrey! How kind of you, I am sure. Is the music not lovely tonight?' And she'd giggle and flirt, and she'd take another drink." "Imagine!" mused Leslie Cobb. "At eighty-four." "Then she'd order me back to the house. And I'd have to sit and wait for her. I'd come out here now and then -see how she was. But oh, my; if she saw me! She talked dreadful to me as I never, never heard Sophia Pollard talk in all the years I was with her. where she picked up the words. 

Goodness knows 

And she'd sing too-don't know where ever she picked up those songs either, I'm suresure" 

Leslie Cobb asked: "What songs?"

The old woman held her hands against her cheeks in a gesture of being ready to stop her ears. "I couldn't tell you."

"Go on." "I can't tell you. Too awful."

Almost to herself Leslie Cobb said, "I'm beginning to like Aunt. For the first time!" "Then she would-oh! Mrs. Cobb, it was quite awful -she'd drink that vile champagne. And I'd peek out, seeing her stand up very straight, like this." Miss

Plunkett stood up, assumed her version of a duchess on parade-"And she'd walk out on that empty walk there. 

It's a wonder somebody didn't see her. Nothing on but that bathrobe! But the water out there's full of snags and rocks and shoals, they say. So boats don't come close in. . . . And she'd walk 

And she'd walk up and down with that dirty-well, it was dirty!-bathrobe hanging to her absolutely unclothed body (it just hung from a cord at the neck) and she'd sing and act shocking-wiggling herself like she was dancing—and sing like this." 

To Leslie Cobb's astonishment Miss Plunkett raised her cracked voice in song. The words, from her, sounded more bawdy than any Leslie Cobb ever had heard around the Place Pigale or on Chicago's black-and-tan South Side. "Lottie Collins has no drawers, Will you kindly lend her yours?" 

Leslie Cobb started to laugh, put her fingers to her lips, nodded in time to Miss Plunkett's song- "For she's going far away

To sing Ta-ra-ra Boom-de-aaye.. "Why, Miss Plunkett!" 99 "Well, that's what she sang. And lot more just asas-" She couldn't find the right word. "And I had

to live through that awful goings-on every night we were out here." She threw up her hands. “If I didn't who would? Not her kith and kin, not that Whittle- No, I couldn't leave her. She was like your own sister who dwelleth in the wilderness." "About last Sunday night. What'd she do?” 

Miss Plunkett seemed to think her perverse. "Why, same's I told you! After the family left she came out here. She said: "Why, my dear! All my dances are taken tonight. I simply must go tonight.' She railed at me something dreadful; I thought, "This is the last.

I can stand no more of it.' I told her a fog was coming in-'It'll chill you,' I said but what could I do? She couldn't wait for me to open a bottle." 

Leslie Cobb interrupted with a question. "Me? Why, I went back to the house to wait for her,”

Miss Plunkett said. "I always sat in the living room reading or sewing, waiting for her. That night I was afraid she'd catch her death of cold; I could see the fog from the windows. So about midnight I came out here. 

She was singing and talking-(singing that Lottie Collins thing, it was her favorite)—and holding up her glass of champagne, saying, ‘A toast to the bride, then we dance. A toast to the bride!' So I didn't let her see me.' 

"Well, what did you do?" "Went back. Maybe you remember that Sunday night? 

Bright moon 'cept for the fog." "I remember." "Well . . . I suppose I was dozing a bit―(she was always up fresh as a daisy at seven every morning, no matter how late she was out here). . . I woke up. 

Seemed to think I'd heard something. You know? I

Many's the time she stayed later, much later, and if she wasn't so drunk I c'd talk her into coming to bed, she'd scold me into getting her more to drink.

Leslie Cobb shivered. The little pavilion seemed unpleasant and damp. She lighted a cigarette. "And there was fog? And a moon?" "Yes there was." "Fog, like aluminum dust?-falling from an aluminum moon? Oh, well; go on!" "Well. She was lying right there-by that table. 

You could touch the spot with your foot this minute.

I saw what it was. She was bleeding in the head, she was dead. She looked so little, lying there." "And then?” ""Then'?" Miss Plunkett seemed surprised. "Why, I was simply out of my mind. I didn't know what to do, where to turn. I'm not the emotional sort." 

Leslie Cobb sighed. "Well I knew—as if I'd read it in my Bible that I've had since I was baptized, a girl of thirteen, up at Deep River-well I know what I'd have to do if Sophia Pollard wasn't a-going to the grave in scandal and terrible disgrace. She was dead. I couldn't do anything about that. But her good name was dear to me. She meant everything to me." She blew her nose in her handkerchief. "I carried her back to the house-up to her room -and I came back here, cleaned up everything. When 

I was picking up those champagne bottles I sort of lost my head and I threw them as hard as I could upon the rocks down there." "I'll look for them tomorrow." "Eh? Well, so I went back. Remember I kept saying, 'She's a Christian woman, Lucy Plunkett, she's a Christian woman.' Like that. Over and over-" "See here, wasn't she bleeding?” "My, yes. And I washed it all up. And that bathrobe

I myself washed out and boiled after the funeral, it's in a drawer in my room right this minute. And I stuffed

cotton from the medicine cabinet in those wounds in her head." "But Miss Plunkett-after you carried the body to New York- 

"I fooled them. I fooled all of them. That idiotic 

Dr. Spoors-'Oh, my yes; her blood's so thin, the champagne is quite tonic, my dear Miss Plunkett!'-when I phoned him he wasn't in, but later, about 5:30, he came to the house. He had on evening dress. Suppose he'd just got home from some society spree." 

Miss Plunkett stopped, and when Leslie Cobb looked at her it seemed in the dim light she was grinning. "Why, he was easiest of all. He just looked at her, feels her wrist, puts his head to her chest. 'Heart failure' he said to me-Confidentially, and it won't go in the death certificate, Miss Plunkett, it's acute alcoholism.' And he sailed right out of the room. "So," Miss Plunkett said, "I dressed her in some very nice things. Then I called everybody." She seemed to linger over one detail. "The undertaker man, he was the one I feared. But pshaw, he wasn't any bother to me. I fixed her hair. She looked so sweet, like a little girl, you'd never imagine- 

"Wait. You took her to the Fifth Avenue house, got her to her bedroom. Didn't the servants see you?" "No. Nobody." "You had a key, I suppose. But didn't you waken anyone?" "Nobody was in that house when I got there." "Hm! . . . Find any firearms around here? For instance, a Colt automatic pistol?" "No, I did not."

Tuesday A. M., June 9th

In the bare, thin-carpeted law office of Titus R. 

Whittle-Whittle, Sackett & Whittle—that is, behind his private office furnished at the start of the century, there was an L-shaped room lined with law books. Abraham 

Lincoln wore a mantle of dust on top of a pile of daily law reports.

The heirs of Sophia Pollard gathered around an oak table. A chandelier, time-weary, lighted the room so badly that everyone seemed grief-soaked and melancholy. 

The cigarettes made a lot of smoke.

Leslie Cobb was biting her wide full underlip. She did not talk much. Seemed to prefer looking at the others. Her eyes were greyer, more sphinx-like if anything. Yet they wavered with tears. She watched John

Phelps poke among some dusty ore specimens on a shelf, then she pushed back her chair, and her sable scarf snaked to the floor. 

She spoke to the younger Whittle. "Ricks." Her lips hardly moved. He turned and she mouthed the words: "want-to-see-you," and walked casually to the other end of the L-shaped library. "Anybody in your office?" "No," Ricks Whittle said. "Come on in.”

They closed the door on the others. He began: "Leslie, I want to apologize about the way I acted on John's boat. 

I was a swine." "Forget that." Then she hesitated. "Ricks, I—I don't know where to turn! I've got to get your advice. Itit's ghastly." "What's wrong!

She glanced toward the closed door. They could hear 

Eleanor Duane and Margery Phelps talking. She was

barely breathing. “Ricks—she didn't 'just die.' I tried to tell you on John's boat." He asked her what she meant. "She didn't even die at the house in town." 

She could not speak fast enough. "She was killed by somebody, Ricks." 

His upper teeth bit into his lip. "Sophia Pollard? 

That's absurd." But-he couldn't avoid her anguished face-"Eh? How do you know? Eh?" She couldn't answer for a minute, and he said, "How do you know?" 

She said: "I don't know, much. I know that!"

It seemed Ann Phelps was restless out in the library, and she walked past the elder Whittle and the others, opened Ricks Whittle's door, looked in. "Oh, I'm sorry," she said, looking slowly at Leslie 

Cobb and Ricks. She backed out. 

Leslie Cobb waited; then breathlessly she spoke and 

Ricks said "Good God!" Once he asked: “And Plunkett was the only one who heard the shots?" "So she said. And all that Midnight Mystery stuffsays she was to save the family honor. As if it mattered." He asked a question. "Ricks! How do I know?

We have only her word. But she's right, we've got to keep this mess on the Q.T.-until we know something definite." "I check with that," he said. "But it all seems darn queer-why, you'd think somebody would've seen bullet wounds- There was a moment of deep silence. 

They watched one another helplessly. 99 "Well," Leslie Cobb said. "Being as I'm the one who's stumbled into it" "We'll put "No, no!" it up to Father," he said. "Yes, Leslie. Father'd-"

"I said no, not yet. Haven't I done pretty well on my own? I intend to have a look-'round, first. You must say nothing. Promise?" "All right. I'll tell you. We-you and I'll go ahead.

If you need me if I can do any damned thing at all- 

I'm all with you. And you with me?" "Right." Disturbed in thought she asked: "Shouldn't we say anything to Neville or John?" He was opposed to that just yet. "I think you're right,” she said.

The telephone was ringing. A stenographer opened the other door, struck Leslie Cobb in the side. “Oh,

I'm so sorry," she said, and spoke to Ricks Whittle who said, "Come on, Leslie."

They went back to the library. He spoke to his father. 

The elder Whittle cleared his throat: "My assistant has phoned. He is downtown filing the will. As the personal counsel—and, if I may add, the old and bereaved friend-of the late Sophia Pollard, I shall open for you the last will and testament of said testatrix, said testament having been drawn by me at her direction, and we shall also give an accounting of sundry investments and expenses- 

John Phelps sat on the radiator behind him. "Oh, cut all that, Whittle," he said. His eyes were bloodshot. 

Whittle coughed. "Beg pardon?" "Get it over with, man. This room's hot. Open it, read it, let us know what we get. That's what we're here for." "Very well, John," said the lawyer. He adjusted his tortoise-shell spectacles, began reading.

CHAPTER V  

Tuesday P. M., June 9th

THE big car came up Newfield, climbed a hill and stopped at Leslie Cobb's place. It looked cool and sheltering, after the trying morning in New York. Margery 

Phelps' chauffeur opened the door. 

Leslie Cobb asked: "Wonder if you'll be home this afternoon?" The chauffeur waited. He could see the white underside of her leg. "Why, yes," Mrs. Duane said. "But ought you to move around yet?" "I may call you, Eleanor." "You ought to go ri-ight to bed. Come for tea? You come, Margery." 

Margery Phelps murmured, "Thanks heaps. I've a fearful headache. It was all so distressing at Whittle's office." 

Leslie Cobb said: "You must be a wreck-listening to your husband inherit ten million dollars or so." "Oh, Leslie. How can you talk that way?" "Don't mind her!" "Good-by." Leslie Cobb got out. "See you later, Eleanor." 

Margery Phelps' car moved up Newfield. saw Valery idling in a swing under a tree. 

Leslie Cobb 

Miss Blythe was in a chair reading. Valery saw her mother and 74

jumped to the ground. "Dearest," the child said, “I've missed you so. What did you bring me?" 

Leslie Cobb kissed her. "Nothing this time. But don't worry. Your mother's a rich woman now." She fingered the child's light hair. “Oh, so nice and rich.” 

Valery said, "Yes?"

Leslie Cobb walked toward the house. She stooped and picked a copper red columbine, bit off a long fragrant spur, chewed it. Thus, thoughtful, she went upstairs to her room, where she put some color on her lips and fell back upon the bed.

It was forty-five minutes later that she stirred, yawned like a Siamese cat, drew up her knees, turned on her side with her head on her right wrist. Suddenly she sprang to her feet and started to undress. 

There was a mirror in the door to the cedar closet. 

She looked at herself. "Nice thoughts you've got, eh?" she asked. "Young slut." 

Then she ran to the shower.

Shortly before four she went downstairs. She saw the cook. "Mrs. Sundquist,” she said, “if I get any telephone calls I'll be at Mrs. Duane's. Here! I'll write down the number."

She went out to her car, swung it north up Newfield, a concrete highway running between smart estates, drove almost two miles, turned in between whitewashed rocksand scraped the right front fender. "You're a fool, you ought to be in bed," drawled 

Eleanor Duane. She had a cigarette in her hand. She was a chain-smoker and always inhaled. She stood on the steps of a large rambling country house sheathed

with white shingles. The window shutters were greenish-black. "Your gate-post scratched my fender," Leslie Cobb said. "Pah, I can go to town now and get a new Ford. I can get a dozen new Fords, can't I? I can get a ten-ton lorry if I choose. Or a lovely Moth. .. I feel like those Oklahoma Indians who buy a new Packard every time they get drunk and ditch the old one." "Leslie! You talk like a half wit."

Eleanor led the way to an umbrella on a terrace laced with snapdragons. From around the house a maid came with the tea things. She was humming a song but stopped when she saw them. "I lo-athe harmonizing wenches," Eleanor said. "How are you feeling? What a shock you gave us. First Jack gets blown up, then you drown. Why didn't you answer my letters? Gone four years and only sends Christmas cards!" "Oh, letters are such torture for me." "I tell you, Leslie, John makes me tired. Hear him this morning? You'd think there was a Black Hand bomb plot against young Jack. Like you read about. 

Why, everybody says Jack was monkeying with those photographers' things. But no, John insists it was some villainy by God knows whom." 

Halfway through a cup of tea Leslie Cobb said: "You put on a good show at the funeral." 

Mrs. Duane drank her tea. "-As if we didn't all," said Leslie Cobb, "know how you hated her. Oh, don't be snouty, Eleanor. You did hate her."

Mrs. Duane chewed into a sandwich. "All right," she said. "Suppose you didn't?"

A cocker spaniel came up to examine Leslie Cobb, who leaned over and shook the proffered foot. "Gorgeous feathers," she said, adding-"Aunt's life was not amusing. All she did was play the magnificent miser. Then she died—" The cocker cringed happily. "The breed's bigger in England," she remarked. Looking up she said, "P'r'aps I didn't like her! Her corsets never fit. She reminded me of a math. prof. I had at Wellesley." "You've gone craazy," Mrs. Duane said. "Is it very hot in Africa?" "Rather." Leslie Cobb leaned across the table. "Seriously, how much did you hate Aunt Sophia?" 

Mrs. Duane took it in her stride. "I'm glad she's dead." Into the other's silver-grey eyes she said: “And 

God knows you are too, Leslie. Didn't she try to wreck your life too? Didn't she slap us down if we found a bit of happiness?" "Oh, she didn't do so much to me," Leslie Cobb said; "she only ran me out of the country."

Mrs. Duane shook her head, excited with her thoughts. "I'm your sister and I'm older. But I'll be damned if I understand you, Leslie. You're too flip." "Flip," said Leslie Cobb. "Flip and flop. Flip-flop. 

Flop and flip- 99 "Ah-h!" Mrs. Duane's scorn was almost a scream. 

She broke three pieces of cake in her fingers and threw the bits on the table. She came back to her thoughts. "There were times when I could've killed her." "Did you?" Leslie Cobb asked. "Did I what?” "Kill her."

As Mrs. Duane started to get angry again Leslie Cobb added, “Well, couldn't somebody have? How do we

know she wasn't? We've only Plunkett's word for it that Aunt died in her sleep." "That Plunkett," Mrs. Duane said. "Meeching, meeching. That Bible-thumping old virgin. She'll go packing now, let me tell you.' 

"Who else don't you fancy?" "Leslie, stop it. You can't understand how I feel or you wouldn't egg me on. I'm so-o bitter inside I'm gall. 

My life's over. I'm forty-three. Everything-any happiness I might have had—all burned out." She lifted tragic eyes. "She burned it out, she spoiled my life"-a twisted laugh. "Do I hate her?" She turned away to the maple trees beyond the lawn. "Snooping clam-mouthed Yankee she-devil hoarding her millions. Nagging at us children. 

Nagging. Tyrannizing over me all my life. How I lo-athed her thin selfish lips. . . . Oh, Leslie, you don't know how it's been. You got away for four years." "Exiled. For love." "Oh, shut up. Suppose you did go around with that 

Englishman, or sleep with him, or whatever you did!

precious. All is not lost. Emotionally you're still a debutante."

A clown with a breaking heart.” "About Sunday night. Didn't you go back-later?"

set for one of her jags and she had no time for my troubles." "Jags'?" "That was no secret, the whole family knows it. She drank like a fish." She rushed on-"Spoors started it. 

Prescribing brandy and milk for her blood. But then that Whittle-yes, and Neville got keen about it tookept sending her cases of booze. Cases! She drank all the time, Leslie. Very secretive, our fine Aunt, but I knew all about it."

Leslie Cobb made a scribble with her finger on the "E. P. D." embroidered in the center of her napkin. "Well!" she said. "That's out!" She said to Mrs. Duane, "I'm checking you off my list." 66 As Leslie Cobb sped out of the house she met Miss Blythe bringing Valery home from a party at the Louis

If servants chose to be so impertinent back home they'd be jolly well chucked out before- 99 But Leslie Cobb by now was in her little car again, and rolling downhill between the green-soft country estates, streaking toward Stamford. 

At Shorecrest, brakes creaking, she stopped before the garage. Johnny Smith, in rubber sea-boots, was washing a 1922 Rolls. "Hello, Johnny. When're you going to dunk my Ford?" "And besides," he said, "I'll simonize it"-adding, "for you, Mrs. Cobb."

She sent the car forward under the poplars and out on the sandspit and up to Sophia Pollard's house. In her day Sophia Pollard never allowed cars to drive up to her house.

Out of the corner of her eye Leslie Cobb noticed the woman cook and the chambermaid peeking at her from a window opening on the porch. She found Miss 

Plunkett walking in front of the fireplace. "How're you?" She lighted a cigarette. 

Miss Plunkett turned. She looked older. "I've been trying to get you!" She tiptoed to both the pantry and a door leading to the sewing room where the two women hid.

"This is mysterious," said Leslie Cobb between cigarette puffs. 

Miss Plunkett sat beside her on the settee in front of the fireplace. "Oh, Mrs. Cobb!" "Well, what now?" "The house has been robbed and they've taken some of her jewelry, some old pieces. They were valuable, real lovely- "When did it happen?" "How should I know, Mrs. Cobb? I found them missing when I was going through her room this morning.... Mr. Whittle ordered me to clean up her things and I was doing merely as he said... she had a drawertop drawer-in her bureau in her bedroom, full of these things. Old pieces of jewelry-carnelian earrings and gold chains and two Swiss watches with rubies, some diamond bracelets, all very out of date, I imagine, but very pretty." 

Miss Plunkett puckered her lips. "I think, Mrs. Cobb, your Aunt was becoming childish. She insisted on keeping this big lot of jewelry in the drawer-oh, it was quite a heap and she would take them out and put them on every day. Like a girl with dolls." "You say they are missing?" "Oh, but that's not all. There's a chest, a heavy antique chest, in her room too. Don't suppose you remember what her room looks like?" 

Leslie Cobb said, “Vaguely.” "Well. She had that chest just plumb full of things.

Hind wheels chattered in the gravel-spun—and she was gone.

Leslie Cobb was having her coffee on the east porch. 

John Phelps, summoned by telephone, sat in his riding

clothes on the arm of a chair. He took off his coat. "Hot," he said. She noticed a bandage through his shirtsleeve. The bandage was on the fleshy part of his left arm, near the vaccination-mark area. "Hurt yourself, John?" "Nope. Boils." He asked about her again. "Don't worry about me," she said. "I tell you I feel quite all right, John. I don't drown that easily." "That was a hell of a place for a woman to start fainting. If you felt that way-why didn't you go below to one of the cabins?" 

For a minute she stared. . . . "See here, John. Ever run into one Gus Papageorge?-Sounds like the hero of 

Valley Forge but this one's Greek." 

He smoked his cigar. "Why"-rolling smoke from his lips-"you mean that fellow who runs the speakeasy in Stamford?" "No, not that one. Brother Gus, gloomy Gus." She looked at him. "Been to see you? Lately? And was the word 'pregnant' brought in the conversation?" 

It was half a minute before he replied. "I get you. 

My answer is 'Yes' and 'No.' Yes, he came to see me. 

No, he did not use such a swanky word as 'pregnant.'"

I'd thrash the daylights out of him." "D'he get your feathers up?" "He's a mournful buzzard. Tall and hungry-looking.

I am-a father suffering like hell.' Stuff like that." "What did you do, John?" "And I said to him, 'See here, Bosco, you don't know what suffering is! But you will! He said, 'He ruin my girl. I make you pay.' Then I said, 'Now you get the hell out of here, and be snappy about it! I'm tired of this nonsense."" "What did he do then?" There was a young chap at the wheel. Foreign-looking.

Piece of tin cut Jack's mouth. There were pieces of thin metal embedded in the walls, in the hall."

John Phelps smoked his cigar. "Europeans have odd codes," Leslie Cobb said, as if thinking aloud. "I once motored through Greece. A sultry people, brooding over the lost Atlantis. Touchy about money, honor, personal wrongs; things like that." 

She watched him narrowly. 

He grunted. Finally his eyes met hers. "Well?"

Leslie Cobb lifted the percolator from the electric plate, sighed. "Haven't had good coffee in years. Think that's why I came home. Think I'll go on a coffee stinko.”

She poised the spout invitingly over another cup. He shook his heavy head, studying her. She asked indolently, "Haven't forgotten our little talk on your boat, have you?" "You bet I haven't." 

She said: "It's my crazy idea. I want you not to forget that either. For the time being I want to sift it alone. 

That agreed?" 

He reflected, watched her, said, "Yes." 

She said: “I need something to do. You know how women are, now. And running down a murder-plus maybe attempted murder-right in your family is more thrilling than running a highfalutin antique shop." The blink of her eyelids indicated that her nerves were more on edge than she realized. "This is too cockeyed for any sane man!" he burst out.

Surprisingly, his cosmopolitan manners fled, he talked like an oil-field toolie. "My God, Leslie, what're you yapping about anyway? If you've got something-spill

What do ... you think we'd better do? 

The police"

John Phelps said, "It's a terrible thing-to accuse a man of murder. But I see it all now. He killed Aunt and tried to kill Jack when I still held out.-See him tonight, Leslie? CHAPTER VI  

"THIS IT?" "Yes, ma'am."

Leslie Cobb beheld the house on her left. It stood on a terrace moody with a few canna lilies and weeds. It overlooked a marshy neck of the Sound holding coal pockets and machine shops. "These poor Greeks pick their scenery," she said. "A place with a view'." "They buy it cheap around here. They and the Italians." "Well, you stay here." Leslie Cobb got out, went up the cement steps to the porch. In a faded hammock sat a tall, hollow-eyed man. He had handle-bar mustaches. His eyebrows were not as heavy as his mustaches. He was hitting at mosquitoes with a swatter.

A stout middle-aged woman sat in a kitchen chair near him. "Good evening," Leslie Cobb said. The man muttered in Greek beneath his huge mustaches. The woman stood up and said, "How do?" When Leslie Cobb asked for 

Gus Papageorge the woman tilted her head. Leslie Cobb walked up to him and said: "I am Miss Howard of the 

Fresh Air Fund." 

The man was in his undershirt and trousers. He smelled of sweat. The sweat was like Kleinert's shields 93

on his armpits. He spoke impatiently to his wife. She answered him in Greek. "What you want?" she asked Leslie Cobb. "We are sending several hundred children on a twoweeks' outing in July. I wondered if your daughters would like to go?" 

Now the stout woman said, "Ah! Yes, yes." She turned to explain to Papageorge, who shrugged. His wife was excited. She ran to the doorway and screamed, "Panagota. Come here, Panagota."

A girl of about fourteen came to the door and looked at Leslie Cobb. Her mother talked to her. The girl said: "She says, how much will it cost?" "Oh, not a cent. It won't cost anything. Would you like to go?" 

Panagota said, “Sure. Where is it, Candlewood Lake?" 

Her mother was talking again and the girl said, "Sit down, please."

Leslie Cobb took the kitchen chair near Papageorge. 

On an impulse she opened her purse and drew out a thin notebook with a small gold pencil stuck on the side. "Now," she said to him, "may I ask how many of your children could go on the holiday?"

He shrugged, spoke to Panagota, who said: "He says, I can go." He spoke in grunts to the girl. "And he says Athena and Alene." The mother was interrupting. 

The girl said: "But Nicolina, she can't go." The mother shook her head apologetically and said, "No. No, Nicolina." The girl said: "Ma says she's the baby, so Nicolina can't go."

Old Papageorge muttered. His wife spoke to him and he became more disturbed. The girl said: "Pa says Penelope, she can't go neither."

Leslie Cobb had been told that her eyes were very candid and without guile. So she looked at Papageorge candidly and without guile. "Why can't Penelope go?"

He looked past the clematis vine, toward the water and the great gasoline tanks and the cars going by down on the street. Sullenly he spoke to Panagota, who hesitated, then said to Leslie Cobb: "He wants to know what-for you come at this time? He says the health nurse used to come in the day." "Why-you see- 

Papageorge nodded up and down. He talked to his wife, to Panagota. A girl younger than Panagota came to the door. The mother yelled, shook her hands at her. 

The girl ducked back in.

Leslie Cobb explained that the Fresh Air Society was short of workers and since she happened to be in the neighborhood she stopped in. 

Papageorge said: "All right, lady; come tomorrow." "Oh, you speak English?" 

He waved a negligent hand. "Not good. Excuse me." 

Then a girl came up the front steps. The mother ran to the steps, spoke vehemently. Papageorge looked at his watch. It was in a little pocket and fastened to his trousers with a cord of braided leather shoelaces. 

The girl was not swart like the others. Her small face was of classic outline. Her eyes were small and close together. She wore pretty clothes. As she started in the house she saw Leslie Cobb. Now Papageorge spoke to her. His face twisted as if he suffered, and as he talked his wife threw in a few words. Their jabber sounded like bricks falling down a housewrecker's chute. . . . . . But the girl lifted a shoulder, went inside. "What a beautiful daughter you have, Mr. Papageorge."

His smile was malevolent. "How you know my name?" He turned to his wife. She replied in Greek spattered with English. He said to Leslie Cobb: "Please, you come tomorrow, eh?" He went inside. Leslie Cobb heard him yelling. The adenoidal Penelope answered. 

Leslie Cobb heard her say, “Ah, lay off me, can'tcha?” 

The father raised his voice and Penelope said, "I was only down to Mary's. Shut up your fuggin yapping."

The mother, on the porch, was sniffling and Leslie Cobb stood up. "Well, I must go." She was halfway down the steps when Papageorge ran out of the house, his voice mottled with rage. He said something to Panagota. 

She called: "Hey, what you say your name was?"

Leslie Cobb had forgotten; she turned so her words would be indistinct. "Miss . . . ." "Wait!" yelled Papageorge. He started down the steps.

Leslie Cobb went down the rest of the steps, climbed into the car. She said: "Let's go. I found out what I wanted." She stepped on the starter but the motor didn't catch. Papageorge was calling to her, waving an arm. Johnny Smith pulled the choke and it caught. The 

Greek was at the bottom of the steps yelling.

The car moved. "He's just a harmless old Greek," 

Leslie Cobb said, shifting. “Doesn't know what it's all about." 

As they jerked forward Johnny said, "I could've told you that, ma'am." She swung the car around a corner. "That all you wanted me for?" he asked.

She was biting her lip as she drove. They went almost two blocks before she said: "I'd like to meet his brother. 

That one that runs the speakeasy. It's near here?"

Pete's place stood between a neighborhood bakery and a marine hardware store catering to local fishermen.

Leslie Cobb followed Johnny Smith. The fly-speckled aisle was narrowed by counters and two open barrels of black olives. A young Greek stood up to let her pass. 

He chewed a match, his shoe-button eyes on her. She gave an appearance of indifference as she looked at the cans of sardines, olive oil, pimiento, at hard black sausages hanging from ceiling and store window, at long loaves of bread stacked beside a worn cash register. 

She was conscious of a man, a layered bulk of a fat man with wide greasy nose and greasy cheeks, who filled the enfilade to the rear of the store. “Hello,” he said. He belched with effort. "Evening, Pete. How's everything, Pete?" To her astonishment Johnny Smith was meek as a beggar. The blubberous person's head was stiffened so he could look at her beyond Johnny's shoulder. Johnny said: "It's all right, Petey." "This is delicatessen store. What you want to buy?" "It's O. K. Who, her? Why, she's Mrs. Cobb. Kin o' Pollard's, yeh."

Leslie Cobb walked forward, and Peter Papageorge had to back into the space between the end of the counter and a shelf holding a few tins of olive oil. He said, "All right." He had the hoarse voice of a fat man. 

She went past him, pushed a door into a room thick with the odors of kitchen and stale ale. Sawdust was on the floor. Two long tables, bare, wooden and muchscrubbed, faced wall benches with black imitation leather cushions. The radio was on. 

A little man with a white apron asked: “What will it be, please?" 

Johnny Smith said, “Roosta bif, ippla pie.”

Leslie Cobb dug her elbow into him and Johnny said:

"Well... vino, George? Vino red? Yeh, I guess we'll have a bottle." The waiter left and he whispered, "I guess we ain't what you'd call friends." "Who? Oh, I see." "Guess Pete wouldn't of let me in if you wasn't along. 

Him? Oh, he thinks I gypped him on a boat I sold him for his hooch pullers. That was more'n five year ago." He grinned, elbow on table, chin in hand.

A man came in. He sat at the table across from them. 

She asked: "Is this the only room he has?" "I'll ask George. Why, that's the waiter, you know. 

Greek waiters is George mostly. Thought you'd been in Europe?" 

The waiter came from a door facing them. She could see the back of a man washing dishes at a sink. Johnny asked, "There any other rooms, George?" 

The waiter's black eyes mocked Leslie Cobb. He said, "Sure. Other rooms." He took an uncorked bottle from his tray, inclined his bushy head. "O.K. in there?" 

Johnny turned to Leslie Cobb, who said, "Let's!"

The waiter shrugged, led them into the kitchen. The pearl diver was slopping dishes, his back to them, singing "-plays the rhumba on the tuba down in Cuba." They turned to the right into another room. It looked like the first one. "You like this?" 

Leslie Cobb said, "Oh, this is fine." She barely sipped the wine. Her stomach still was shy from the salt water.

She worked languidly. The Russian's eyes were like an artist's oil color called "Italian pink"-shining and brown, but with an insincere lustre peculiar to "Italian pink."

Neville Phelps sat on the wall-bench by the window.

Leslie Cobb went on: "So he was taken to a big hospital in Dartford. Dartford's a lovely place. In Kent, you know. Near London. The hospital was run by some ill-mannered American doctors-from Brooklyn, I think. And close by there was a German officers' hospital-prisoners. Now for the story.'

So one night this young leften'nt—" 

"Lieutenant," Neville said. "You're driving me nuts.' "-said he slipped through some flower gardens in the rear of the place, scrambled under a hole in the fence, made his way circuitously to the road to Dartford. "It was a June night, and rather dark. Hardly any-

body on the road. He had on wrinkled hospital blues. 

And to his delight he saw a country girl walking. She was coming toward him. He waited for her, turned in step. She was just a common-looking creature, but he didn't mind. He said, 'Hullo' or 'Pretty evening, pretty maid.' Something like that. "She didn't answer. Hurried on. But he was quite romantic that evening-" Leslie Cobb turned candidly to the Countess: "Russians are romantic, aren't they? 

Even when they're tragic? Or bored? Yes. . . . Well, this girl ignored him, he became quite enamoured, you see? He threw his arm around her, but she shook her head and said 'No, no'-in a strange muffled voice that puzzled him. Sort of guttural voice. But he was quite love-sick, you see? He stopped her, took her in his arms and she mumbled 'No, no!' and he kissed her. Quite violently, there in the road. She struggled away, her head lowered, and he let her go. He said he had quite a troubled impression of it ever since the War." "What's this, Leslie? An Uncle Robert bedtime story?" "Now, Neville. Here's the amazing thing about it:

This American chap told us that it wasn't 'till a couple of years ago he struck the answer." "What answer?" He was glaring at her. "Why! To that girl's conduct, Neville. . . . It wasn't a girl at all! It was one of the German prisoners, a wounded officer, sneaking out of his hospital, playing hookey in clothes he'd probably bribed a scrubwoman to lend him. This American said, 'Wouldn't it be strange if this German and I had been facing one another on the same Front!' He said he'll never get those kisses out of his mouth.

There's a moral in it somewhere.

Two women came in. The highballs had made their faces ruddy and blotched. "-powder on my nose," one was saying. They looked at the two-Countess Pflomm, so slight, so feline and pale and exotic; Leslie Cobb, tall, horsey and weather-stained, the American woman of "good breeding," no prettier than the average but made exceptional simply because of those grey-pigmented eyes. 

The two newcomers performed their rites with considerable chatter across the stall partitions; came out; pulled down their clothes; fixed their faces, left. The

Cobb was much the stronger of the two. The sweat was a fine mustache on Leslie Cobb's upper lip. She took the pistol from the Countess, who smiled and spit in her face.

Leslie Cobb stood back, wiping her face uncertainly.

After speaking to the houseman and to the gardener she drove away in her car. 

At Shorecrest she drove past the main entrance, continued to the service gate. She wheeled to the left, between evergreens that led into two fields of young 

Golden Bantam corn, then grape arbors. She stopped the car and walked through a brushwood path until she came to a sandy clearing near the marshes. 

Old Abel sat in the doorway. The shanty's white paint was scarred with age. Some chickens pecked in the path. As she came up he looked at her over a pannikin of tea held to his lips with both hands. "Well... Abel." She recoiled from the smell oozing out from the hut doorway. "Remember me? I'm Leslie. Aunt Sophia's niece. Used to pelt you with stones when I was a girl. Remember?"

He did not answer. He was a chunky little old man, unkempt and soil-grimed. His lower lids sagged so that she saw the red membrane. "Can you hear me, Abel?" 

He drank tea. "Why shouldn't I?" "I see you're having your eleven o'clock brekkus. You

remind me, Abel, of old men in England, on the farms. 

They simply must have their tea and bread and cheese at eleven, too. Why don't you make them give you your tea up at the kitchen, Abel?”

He laughed. The phlegm rattled in his throat. "Think 

I want to be poisoned?" "Don't you get along with the servants?" "I get along all right. But I got to watch them like a hawk, I do. Every minute. They're trying to rob me. 

They'll not do it; oh, no."

He put the enamel-ware mug on the doorsill and slowly stood up. He had a gardener's stoop. He came close to her. His face was rufous with rusty-red patches. "I know," he said, "why she died!" 

Leslie Cobb said, "Do you, Abel?"

He was chuckling, making exultant noises in his phlegmy throat. For a sly moment he looked at her over his shoulder as, turning, he hobbled inside. She waited. He did not come out; so she went in, but the warm, suffocating stink made her stomach rebel. Old 

Abel was fingering a large padlock on an ancient humpbacked trunk. "Why did she die, Old Abel?" But his eyes were inarticulate and quite insane and his head wobbled with laughter. "Why, Old Abel? Tell me why? You don't think I would poison you?" 

"Ho, no?" It seemed to amuse him. He fumbled in his pocket, drew out a jack-knife. A polished spike lay folded along one end. Jerkily he opened the single blade. He crept toward her. "Old Abel takes care o' himself." He held the knife up at her, near her throat. "You wouldn't hurt me, would you, Abel?”

KIDNAPPED? 117 "Hurt you? Oh, no. But they'd better mind themselves."

Leslie Cobb took the knife out of his hand, shut the blade, gave it to him. He put the knife in his pocket. 

She said: "I don't think that's much protection. What you need is a revolver.” "Eh-yes; a revolver." 

Carefully she asked: "You've got one, haven't you? 

Maybe a nice Colt-an automatic? Let's see the pistol you've got, Abel. Where is it?" He did not say anything. She walked over to the hump-backed trunk. "Is it in here?" He nodded foolishly. "Open up the trunk, Abel." "That's what they all want," he said. "My treasures." 

He added: "Yes, yes,-my lovely treasures. That's why she died. Oh, yes; oh, my, yes. She had all the money from Elijah and Stella and David and Calvin. It all went to her. She held out-waiting for them to die 'n' leave it to her, the bloodsucker. But she couldn't have Old 

Abel's treasures. Oh, no. She'd rather have Old Abel's treasures, his lovely treasures-much's she higgled and haggled and scraggled"-he paused for another one- "and naggled."

He was amused again. “But Old Abel was too good for her. Oh, yes. She wanted his treasures and she sent them to poison him and she died in a stew... frettin' and fumin'. I fought her off. Oh, yes, I did and the hate got in her blood, she couldn't eat with all that hate in her blood- "Open the trunk." “Eh?” "Come on!"

With a cry he fell on his knees and clutched the top of the trunk with his arms and hands. "Stop that noise, Abel. I told you to open that trunk. 

Or do you want me to get Johnny Smith and Wilson down here with an ax?" "You wouldn't do that?" he pleaded. And then with groans and grumbling he took a key from his pocket and put it in the padlock. . . . "There they are! See them! Oh, my lovely treasures. All my lovely treasures!"

The light was faulty. Leslie Cobb leaned over the trunk. "Good God. Why they're nothing but shoeswomen's shoes."

He reached into the trunk. There were sag-heeled house slippers, patent leather pumps misshapen with wear, battered and scuffed shoes with French heels, dusty old high-top shoes; all tied with string, with ribbon or pieces from flannel underwear; all strewn with flowers long since dried. "Abel! Where's the pistol?"

As the train neared Greenwich, wind was blowing dust on the roadbed so that she had to shut her window. 

A man in the chair behind her called to some one across the aisle: "Didn't I tell you? Here's that storm blowing up."

Passing Cos Cob, over the viaduct at the inlet, she saw the clouds gathering black. It began to rain. When the train reached Stamford she took a taxi home. People

on the streets fled from the slanting downpour. The taxi was damp and hot, it smelled of stale cigarette smoke, and the rain pelted the windows. 

The taxi driver turned at the mailbox in front of her house and stopped in the driveway. She paid him and jumped out. Her long legs moved over the grass, she fled toward the side porch. 

The rain fell and it cooled her face; but her back and legs ached. It was quite dark. She paused on the porch. 

Her watch said 6:23. She was going to bed early.

Things were getting very mixed up. She felt depressed. 

The gardener was standing around by the corner of the house, near the old well. When she looked at him he turned and moved out of sight. "Why doesn't that fool get out of the rain? Broken the lawnmower! Or else he's been rowing with Sundquist again."

Sundquist ran out with an umbrella. He held the door for her. "Good evening, madam. I was waiting with the car." "Sorry. Forgot to phone you." She looked at him. 

What in God's name was the matter with that fellow? 

Or was she just cranky at the world tonight? 

Then she saw Miss Blythe. 

Leslie Cobb said: "Something wrong. What is it? 

What's the matter?" Her heart beat swiftly.

She hurried to her bedroom. Fear now seized her body like a spasm. There was a bottle of whiskey about somewhere. She pulled open drawers. Valery! Where, in God's name, was Valery? She pawed at some lingerie. The Colt she'd taken from the Russian touched her fingers. The butt felt cold and reassuring. 

She ran downstairs, eyes staring into the distance. 

Miss Blythe found her in the rain, out at the playhouse.

CHAPTER IX  FOLLOW 

OLLOWING telephonic instructions, Leslie Cobb drove into Stamford. She passed the Ferguson Library, swung northward toward the Boston Post Road. And then Carnley in his car drove alongside he nodded vigorously-and she stopped.

A man stepped out from a darkened store front. She recognized him as Karl. 

Carnley spoke to Karl. "You do the watchdog."

Then he came over to Leslie Cobb's car and lifted the rumble seat hood. He asked her: "You know what to do now?" She shook her head affirmatively. "Keep a dead pan," he warned, and climbed in the rumble and took a revolver from an armpit holster, another from his right hip. "Good you ain't my size, chief,” Karl grinned.

Carnley grunted as with effort he secreted himself inside the rumble. "Oke, chief?" Karl asked and pulled the hood down over Carnley's head. She heard his muffled voice behind her. Karl said to her, "All right, ma'am," and she drove forward.

Now they were on the Post Road. It was a cheerful summer night. As the man on the telephone had said, she came eventually upon dog kennels on her left, the white Country Kitchen with glowing open-air rotisserie, the antique shop on the right with a porch holding huge fat carboy bottles, chests, spinning wheels. 156

Then the filling station. Yes. It was the one. She saw the red and white CARS GREASED sign in flickering neon lights. Her heart pounded, her eyes filmed as she stared ahead.

Cars were swooping past. She put on the brakes, held out a left arm. She tried to turn. A blundering truck came roaring from behind, ground past her signalling hand. Then a brightly illuminated Short Line bus and a string of cars came along on the other side; but at last she was able to swing her car across the road. She drove over the cinders, up to the filling station. 

A man with a patent-leather beak on his white cap came out. "Fill it up?" he asked. 

In a low voice she said: "Five gallons. I don't think it will hold any more." "Six for a dollar?" "Very well."

After he pumped the six gallons he went around to the left of the car, lifted the hood, plucked a thin rod with a middle finger, wiped it, put it back, pulled it out. "Yep, oil's O. K." Then he unscrewed the radiator cap and went for a water sprinkler. He poured water in the radiator, spilled over, wiped the radiator with a rag. 

He came around, wiped the windshield. He was intolerably slow. 

Her voice was rigid and low-pitched. “Was a message left for me?"

He came to the side of the car, beside her. He had tired blue eyes. "You the lady?" he asked. "Yes'm, some fellow left a letter. Said you'd pick it up but I didn't remember the name he said you was." He went in the filling station, returned with an envelope. It was finger-stained. There was no writing on it.

NO! NOI THE WOMANI 

She gave him a dollar, found a quarter in her purse, gave him that. She shifted to first but held the clutch out, looked at the envelope uncertainly; then she drove out to the road, headed south. Carnley was hammering the inside of the car with a revolver butt. She cut the switch and heard him say: "Go in first cross-roadstop.❞

She started the engine again, drove on, her eyes watching the right beyond the curb. Abruptly she braked, wheeled around into a side thoroughfare. Empty weedlittered fields stretched on both sides here. A large building loomed on a hill. It looked like a school house. 

And then a car came up behind them. Karl jumped out, lifted the rumble seat.

Levin? If he ranks this play I'll break his nose. Wasn't he tailing you?" "He was that, chief."

Carnley called: "Can you go through with it, Mrs. 

Cobb?" She nodded to Karl as if she were shivering

with cold. A spasm, like a nervous shudder, moved through her. "Do hurry!" she said.

She started the engine, turned. At the Post Road she turned left. At Darien a policeman with a gun on his 

Sam Browne stood on the curving right curb. Her car's lights picked up his shiny puttees. Ahead, the 

Post Road dipped under a bridge. So she curved right, drove ten feet, stopped. Silver road markers read: Roton

She screwed the speedometer trip numerals back to ooo. The last o was red. . . . She heard Carnley call: "All set?" She drove on....

On her right was a row of stores, and a post office. 

Left, single car tracks, then a railroad embankment.

Now the car dipped downhill, passed the last house, rushed between trees that raised black walls on both sides of the road. Uncounted lights winked at her from the black trees-eyes watching her. The lights flickered uneasily. Yet it was cool with the smell of trees. And the lightning bugs pursued their mates in the night. 

Under the dashlight the mileage numerals moved, relentless, terrifying. She drove with hands and elbows on the wheel. All at once a chill swept her back, her temples. She stared ahead. In the rays of her headlights a touring car lurked ahead, at the right side of the road. 

She looked at the speedometer. It read: 009. The red nine melted down.... It read oro as she put on the brakes. ...

The man held a revolver at her face. He was a young man. She could not see his face clearly, but he was tall, his shoulders bulked. "Get out," he said. "Keep your mouth shut."

She could not move. She was frozen with terror. "You coming out?"

She started to get out on the left. "Get out this side, sister."

Her long legs had trouble getting around the gearshift. She stepped into slanted soft earth. "Where's the money?" "It's on the seat," she said; "there."

He reached in, took the newspaper-wrapped bundle, cut the motor and headlight switches and shoved the revolver against her right side. "Too bad for you if it's phoney," he said. "Go on, ahead." They went forward in the darkness. He warned, "Don't try any fenangling." 

Suddenly a car came up from behind. The driver slowed down, idled his motor, leaned over to say: "Car break down, brother?" 

Leslie Cobb felt the pressure of the revolver relax. 

The young man at her side said: "Huh?" And the motorist said: "Trouble with your car, brother? Maybe give you and the lady a lift?" "That's all right." "Might be able to fix your car, brother, if it ain't too bad, as I'm a pretty good mechanic—" 

The young man at her side said, "Screw, monkey." "Hate to see you walking." 

The young man said: "How would you like to wake up in a pine kimono and find a guy slapping you in the face with a spade?" And he added, "Beat it." 

The motorist laughed, "Excuse me, brother. No offense, I hope? I was only offering to help you and the lady. Well-I'll be moving on. Good night."

He drove on. She could see the tail-light of his car

in the darkness. During this encounter she had not turned her head but she had recognized the voice of the helpful motorist. It was Karl's. 

They came up to the touring car. It had side curtains. It waited in the bay of a small bridge. Shallow water spread to the right among the trees. "All set, Smokie?" "Yeah," said the man at her side. "Let's move, then." 

Another young man, a flutter-fingered person flourishing a flashlight, was standing by the rear door. He had, she saw, pimply eruptions on his face. He pulled the door open for her. At the wheel she saw a third man, thick-set, with ears jutting from a close haircut, but she saw him indistinctly. The motor was running. 

She turned with a sob, leaned into the car. "Where is my daughter? Where is Valery?"

The rear of the car was empty. "Get in, sister." "Oh, but didn't you say- 

"You'll get her. Get in." And the young man who had brought her-the one they called Smokie-gave her a push. She turned to him indignantly. She saw his face, it amazed her. It was angelic, too pretty for a man's; it was the face of a choir boy. "Get in," he said; "we'll take you to her. Don't get excited, sister. Look, we ain't excited, are we? There's no need to get excited, just get in.”

She hesitated-the two men took her elbows. The flutter-fingered, pimply one was much shorter than she was, but Smokie was every bit as tall, and muscular as well. They forced her in and Smokie climbed in after her, he sat at her left. The pimply young man ran

beside her on the right. "We're going to blind- 

He caught a length of around the back and jumped in "Don't be afraid," Smokie said. fold you, just to play safe, see?" cloth around her eyes, tied it in the back. Suddenly he pressed his hand on her mouth. She gasped-he held her mouth. When he took his hand away she could not open her mouth. She put her hand up and felt a large strip of adhesive tape, but by then they had her arms. "Hold her," Smokie said.

From behind them, in the dark road, a headlight came alive. The thick-set driver leaned out to look and the pimply one yelled, "Take it away, Al!" The touring car scuffed forward, the motor coughed. Al, the driver, swore; he threw it into second. The car rushed forward; he shifted to third; the car gained speed. "Ah," Al called over his shoulder, "that was just some bird in a car." "Yeh?" Smokie said.

They came upon two cars halted parallel on the sides of the road. The car on the left stood on the street-car tracks. Neither car had lights, but as the touring car shot between them the two cars roared into action. 

And behind them a car was coming fast. "Shamus!" "Go ahn, you son of a bitch, ride it!"

Al bent to his work over the wheel; the touring car roared down the road. The man at Leslie Cobb's right looked back, his pimply face close to hers. "They're putting a tail on us," he screeched. He had a “foreign” accent, Greek probably. The touring car swung around a curve, throwing her against one of the men. "I ain't lying to you," the pimply Greek yelled, and the car rattled ahead.

"Double-crossed us, eh?" "Oh-no!" she breathed. The tape held her lips; she tore it off. Tore off the eye bandage. Looked at the man on her left. "You're a smart bitch." "Please, please. I'll do anything you say, I'll stop them. 

Yes! I'll stop them. Let me have my daughter. You've got my daughter, haven't you? Oh, please—” 

The car raced. 

He had his hands on her throat. "Don' do that, Smokie," yelled the pimply Greek, and he leaned across her, grabbed Smokie's hands. They struggled, the hands lost pressure on her throat. "For 

Chris' sake, Smokie!" "Ah, she ratted us"-and Smokie struck her. She put her hands to her face and Smokie leaned in front of her to tell the other man what he could do to himself.

A whirring moment; she fell into a mass of weeds and brush, rolled in a sprawling somersault, tried to

rise, fell down and sobbed, and cars raced with a terrific noise past her head. She could hear firing, and she lay in the warm wet foliage and earth.

The touring car hurtled on ahead, and then its rear left tire exploded and the car jerked over into the road, clattered into the car tracks on the left, and Al the driver fought it back into the road. But it got away again as he stepped on the gas. It broke from the road, stumbled on the car tracks, and crashed into the underbrush, scrambled up a hill and finally stopped against a tree. 

Then reluctantly it slid back into the car tracks. "Mrs. Cobb!" 

Carnley was panting for breath. "You all right, Mrs. Cobb?” 66 She lay in the drain ditch. She had rolled over on her stomach. She seemed to like it there. She nodded.

Then Carnley spoke authoritatively to Johnny. "No!" Leslie Cobb said. "We'll not go anywhere until you answer my questions. What's this all about, Mr. Carnley? Who was the man she identified? What is all this hocus-pocus?" Miss Plunkett, beside her, sat like a stone figure. "Well," Carnley said, "we can be moving north, can't we? I'll tell you.' 99 "You mean, back to Stamford?” "Yes, ma'am. Stamford." 

She told Johnny Smith to drive them home. She said to the detective: "Now, go ahead!" "Well," he said. He lighted a cigar. "It's Roy Walters." "And who is he, please?" "Sugarfoot Roy. That's what they call him. I know him like you'd know a college chum, Mrs. Cobb. He's one bad hood. Been a rat, a cokie, package thief, jailbird, a burglar, all his life, and he's still a youngster-" 

He said to Miss Plunkett: "Young fellow you saw?" 

After a moment she said, "Yes, he was." "Mr. Carnley! What are you going to do? Aren't

you going to have the police catch him? Has he got 

Valery? Aren't you going to—" 

He gave her a look. "Sure. Why, sure."

Leslie Cobb could make nothing of that. "But I want you to be a bit more explicit!"

He smoked his cigar. Miss Plunkett opened a window with vigorous jerks. "He's a Bronx boy," he said. 

Leslie Cobb did not speak to him again. Johnny Smith drove up to Ninety-sixth Street on Park Avenue, turned west to Madison, wheeled north. 

Sunday P. M., June 14th

When they rolled over the bridge and headed east on One Hundred and Thirty-eighth Street, Carnley leaned forward. The partition glass still was half-open. ... "Keep on till you hit St. Ann's." He explained to 

Leslie Cobb: "That's a short cut with clear traffic. 

This's a good chance for me to look up his brother. You wanted action, didn't you? He's got a store up that way."

She barely answered him. His manner seemed to annoy her. She looked out the window. 

Miss Plunkett said: "Mrs. Cobb, I feel sort of faintif I could get a cup of tea, somewhere . . . 

"Of course. We haven't had luncheon, have we?"

Carnley spoke up. "You won't get anything decent around here. I know it like a book. There's only Jews and Italians. Wait till we get out on the Post." 

The two women said nothing. After some blocks 

Johnny Smith asked: "St. Ann's around here?" "Keep on. I'll tell you. It's about six more blocks."

In going up St. Ann's Avenue they passed a small park on their right; they rode for several blocks and suddenly

Carnley leaned forward. "Hey, pull in here, will you? 

That's O. K." He explained to Leslie Cobb: "I'm going to step in here. Only be a minute.” 

She shrugged.

He stepped out on the sidewalk and trotted toward a row of stores. Three boys straggled up to the Rolls, stared at the two women. "Jeez, look where that guy is," one boy said, pointing at Johnny Smith. "It's so awfully warm," Miss Plunkett muttered. 

Leslie Cobb exclaimed: "He annoys me dreadfully. I could just as well have stayed in Stamford! I may have had a telephone call." "We all could have," Miss Plunkett said. "Dear me, why didn't this man go to those policemen and get those pictures and bring them out? I could've looked at them in Stamford. I'm not feeling very well- 

They saw Carnley bluster into a store. 

They waited.

When he returned the three boys still dawdled by the car. He put his hand on one boy's head. "Beat it, you punks!" The boy lost his balance and fell on the sidewalk. Carnley stepped in and shut the door. "Go on. Go up to the next block." 

As Johnny Smith drove toward the next corner Carnley said: "Hey, stop here. Stop on this near side, will you?" The car stopped alongside several ash cans.

Across the side-street was a saloon. It had frosted windows, peeling paint, a rounded gold Pilsener sign on each side of the cat-a-corner door with the word "Beer" carelessly painted out. The saloon was at the end of a row of slummy brownstone tenements. 

Next door-that is, up St. Ann's Avenue-was a stationery store with a red enamel sign: United Cigars. In

front were two stands, also painted red, with "New York American" in white stenciling. The stands were dressed with stacks of newspapers. 

Four young men idled by the two red newspaper stands. One of the four was sitting on top of a stand. A half-dozen women with baby buggies moved listlessly and tenement children played on the sidewalk as far up the street as Leslie Cobb could see.

In getting out Carnley stepped on her foot. "Pardon me," he said. "Do be more careful!" she said.

Carnley stood on the sidewalk beside the car. He was looking toward the four young men at the newsstand. 

He walked away, neglecting to shut the door. 

The four young men stared at the Rolls Royce. 

Carnley stepped over the curb, crossed the side-street, and was halfway past the saloon on the other corner when he seemed to pause uncertainly. It looked to Leslie Cobb-whose vision was blurred by the heat wavering up from the street-as if he called out to the four young men; he seemed to jerk back his head as if he wanted one of them to come over and talk to him privately. "What is that man doing now?" Miss Plunkett fretted. "Goodness knows."

Then, through the unreal dreaminess produced by the heat, Leslie Cobb sort of noticed the other three young men watching the fourth one, as if waiting for him to do something. This young man stood facing Carnley with lowered brows. They were about twenty feet apart. The young man spoke hotly to Carnley-he might have been spitting at him... But Leslie Cobb was watching the

scene through half-seeing eyes. Inside, her heart ached for Valery...

Then a noise startled her-a sullen thump of a noise --and when she looked, Carnley was poised on the sidewalk. He hardly moved. And then he bent forward as if he had cramps in the stomach.

The young man held a revolver aggressively; and then he pivoted on his heel and fled into the store. The other three broke and ran up St. Ann's Avenue. One of them fell over a baby buggy. A large woman pushing it sat down on the sidewalk. The young man was dancing about, like a boxer doing his foot work; he dashed after the other two, and the buggy rolled to the curb, careened, and spilled a baby into the street. 

The three young men disappeared into a basement door under some front steps. "Quick, Johnny!" Leslie Cobb shut the door. "Drive up there, Johnny."

He drove the car alongside the saloon. Carnley now had one knee on the sidewalk. His right hand held a revolver. He rose slowly, his left hand propped on his knee; and then he was on his feet. He walked four or five steps toward the stationery store. Then he fell. Leslie Cobb jumped out, ran up to him, put her arms around his body. "Mr. Carnley!" she said. "Mr. Carnley!" He was very heavy. "That son of a bitch," he mumbled.

Then Johnny was helping her; he was straddling the detective's figure and reaching under his armpits. Carnley made an effort to stand and that threw the other two off balance. They nearly dropped him. Leslie Cobb tripped over his legs, said "How stupid!" and caught hold of him. Carnley's revolver fell on the sidewalk.

Leslie Cobb reached around his body, locked her hands under his left arm, her face crushed against his hair. She lifted him up and saw Johnny Smith picking up the revolver and turning toward the stationery store. "No!" she screamed at him. “No, no! Do help me- 

Carnley said, "That son of a bitch." 

Johnny Smith, still holding the revolver, scuttled around behind the car and climbed in across Miss Plunkett's feet. By this time Leslie Cobb had Carnley heaped on the running board. Johnny grasped his arms at the shoulders. The three of them dragged the man up on the back seat, Miss Plunkett pulling the back of his coat. -Easy, sweetheart." Carnley sucked through his teeth. "It hurts in my gut." 66 "We got to take him to a hospital," Johnny was saying. "There a hospital around here?" "Eh? Hospital?"

She turned from the car, saw several men's faces behind the saloon door. She ran up to the door. It had an elaborate cut-glass design in the top half. "Where's the nearest hospital? Where's- 99 They stared at her, moved out of sight inside the saloon, she started to go in. 

But a boy of about fourteen was talking to her. "St. Margaret's” he said. "Four blocks up, lady—you turn right. You'll see it, lady." 

She ran back to the car. "We've got to hurry," she said wildly. "I just know they've been calling about Valery."

It was very hot. She couldn't sleep. She was really too exhausted to get up and take some allonal tablets.

Leslie Cobb returned to her room. At nightfall now the house seemed to creak fitfully; she used to start, get up pistol in hand and in drowsy forgetfulness go to

The Evening Journal man came downstairs from one of the vilely dirty offices maintained by the New York City papers for their police reporters. John Jovino stood in the doorway of his shop where rookie cops bought their $28.75 of nice new harness. "Hot, John.' "9 "You said it, Louie."

The Evening Journal man walked out into narrow, littered Centre Market Place. He stopped two detectives emerging from Police Headquarters across the street. "What's doing?" he asked. "We're anti-Semitic," one of the detectives said, and climbed in a car. "When you get home do me a favor-throw your

mother a bone," called the Evening Journal man, quoting Walter Winchell quoting Dorothy Parker.

Stephano, a detective from Inspector Moriarity's office, came out of the stained greystone building. "What's doing?" asked the Journal man. "Louie, I don't know a thing. I'm going to the ball game. Keep it under your vest." Stephano entered a car, drove down Centre Market Place.

The Journal man went into Headquarters. He climbed the stairs past the police war-dead tablet, turned right, pushed through a heavy iron door, went into an office. "There's Scoops himself," a police sergeant said. "Not the Hot Shot?" another looked up. "Excuse me, gentlemen. Did I say 'Shot'?" "What's doing?" asked the Journal man. After a minute he went out, walked a few steps down the hall, turned in another office. He leaned on a wooden rail and said, "Hello, my pals."

A police clerk sauntered up to him, leaned on the rail. 

He was a man of about thirty-eight or forty, partly bald, with office sallowness on his skin. A few weeks previously the Journal man had arranged for the man's brother to land a clerkship in a Magistrate's court. "Hello," the Journal man said. "Joe getting along 

O. K.?" He lifted a battered pack of cigarettes from his lower vest pocket. "You bet, Louie." The clerk asked, "Want a match?"

He handed him a box. The Journal man opened the box, saw a bit of paper on top of the matches. He took out a match, lighted his cigarette, absent-mindedly stuck the box in his pocket. “Well,” he said, “I got to be ringing doorbells. They

got a new M. E. up at our madhouse and I don't feel like working. I had a bad night." "Yeh ?" "So long." "So long, Louie."

The Journal man went outside, walked to an inside stairway, took out the match box. He read the note.

"Gimme the desk, sweetheart-hurry it for your

The photog pushed his hat back, yawned. “Aah! He's got the crabs off that waitress again."

Thus in the forenoon the big news broke. City editors read damp copies of the Journal extra, remembered that they had buried the shooting of an obscure private detec-

tive named Carnley in the day's Crime story. They reached for direct-wire phones to their men at Headquarters, spoke wistfully. . . . A few minutes later their men, with the Journal man bringing up the rear, attended the 11:30 press conference with the Commissioner. Inspector Michael Moriarity stood at the Commissioner's right elbow. 

An oldish reporter began: "Commissioner, the Journal's got a story-"

The Commissioner hit his desk with his hand. "I know. I know." "What's in it, Commissioner?” "I don't know how in hell that got out." He was somewhat unhappy about it. "But somebody's going to answer me for that. Now that it's out- "Is it true?" "Was Sophia Pollard murdered?" 

"Yes, boys. It was a burglary job. And we're going to break this case quick. We expect to arrest an ex-convict named Walters inside twenty-four hours." “Just a stinking little rat," Inspector Moriarity said over the Commissioner's head. "-We put out an order after midnight. It was marked 

Confidential-No Publicity. That's right, isn't it, Inspector?" "Yes, Commissioner." 

The Commissioner compressed his lips ironically.

And that is the way the Hue & Cry began. It beat in waves upon the family of the late millionaire eccentric; it turned dog-day editions into red-hot sellers; it inspired almost all of New York to look for Sugarfoot Roy Walters.

CHAPTER XI  Monday P. M., June 15th 

AT 2:58 Leslie Cobb reached Stamford. She saw a man get off the train behind her. She took a taxi. The man took a taxi.

As her taxi was climbing the Newfield road hill a quarter-mile from her house, it passed a new roadster in glistening maroon. She leaned from the window: "Hithere!"

The woman driving the new roadster looked around startled, saw Leslie Cobb's head out the window, braked.

Leslie Cobb gave the taxi man a two-dollar bill and jumped out and was almost hit by the taxi bearing the man who followed her from the train. She saw him look at her through the rear window. She walked downhill to the waiting roadster. "Hullo, Margery," she said. "New?" "Yes. Neville bought it for me. It's rather amusing, I think. Especially this yellow suede upholstery. I love to drive myself, it's so much more fun than having a chauffeur. Don't you think so?-Oh! I just stopped at your place but they said you weren't in." "Nice of Neville, wasn't it?" Leslie Cobb put a foot on the running board, a hand on the door. The door was hot and she took her hand away. "Lagniappe for the wifely tears, eh?" 213

Margery Phelps was diminutive and sweet in her summer things. "Leslie, I don't ever understand you. Why should I cry?" "The Countess still lending her sultry charm to your house?" "Tatiana? Oh, she's gone on a cruise to Norway. I do miss her, she's charming. Why didn't you like her?" "I have a snake complex." 

A mood marred the other's face. "Oh, Leslie, you're right, she's vile. I hate her." "Poor Margery." Leslie Cobb patted her arm. "Women who have men around usually come to speak that way of Tatiana."

Margery reddened until her eyes were moist. "How fortunate for you, dear. I mean, that you have no man around- "9 "Oh," Leslie Cobb shrugged, "I know where my two husbands are, I buried them personally. Even so they're hardly safe from Tatiana.”

Margery wet her lower lip and reached to the ignition key. "—Even Norway's not far enough away," Leslie Cobb said. "Margery, I warn you: Tatiana's death on tall handsome Englishmen with straw mustaches."
