At the house she met Miss Blythe at the door. Miss 

Blythe looked pretty haggard. The only call, she said, had been from Mr. Whittle. Leslie Cobb ran upstairs, took a long drink of whiskey, ran to a drawer. She stuffed the automatic pistol in her purse. "If there's any call," she told Miss Blythe, "I'll be back before six." 

She hurried out to the old Pierce. Johnny Smith turned toward Stamford ...

In the bay on the north side of Shorecrest he rowed her

out to an anchored seaskiff. He fastened the rowboat to a buoy; pushed it away, started the seaskiff's engine. "But tell me―" she yelled above the roar. He shook his head, he was busy with the engine. 

The sun blazed down on them. There was hardly any breeze. He steered in a sweeping curve out into Long 

Island Sound, his knee propped against the wheel as he bent down to light an old pipe.

He sent the seaskiff cakewalking through some jibheaded sloops, their kites listless in a faint southwesterly air. The sloops drifted beam-on into nowhere. Then they resented the seaskiff, they shied like frightened ponies. "Hey! You mudscow," yelled a barefoot yachtsman in whites. "Say, what's the idea?" bawled another. "Scratch the stick, boys," Johnny Smith megaphoned with his hands. He grinned at Leslie Cobb; but she sprawled in her seat, eyes thin against the sun. "If they got a reaching breeze,” he said, "they'd yell for Mother." 

He shaded his eyes as he looked at the sky. "And I think that's what they're going to get, too.”

There was another craft near by. "Wish I were on that cutter," she said wearily, nodding toward a deep-keeled, single-masted vessel with dark blue hull. "That's a sloop rig." "Cutter," she said. "No ma'am." "Cutter in Falmouth, England." "This ain't Falmouth, England." "Oh, I don't care! Where're we going?" "Don't know yet, ma'am.” 

An open boat was puffing on their starboard bow. He

said, "Let's see this bum boat feller." When he brought the seaskiff alongside he yelled, "Howdy, Roscoe."

The man in the bum boat raised his hand in greeting and Johnny Smith bawled, "Seen anything of the 

Gypsy?" The other man answered but Leslie Cobb didn't understand, what with the hubbub of marine engines. Johnny Smith notched up his motor, put the wheel over hard and the seaskiff scrambled in a slippery turn across the other boat's bows. 

She asked: "What kind of navigating is that, Johnny?" "I'm in a hurry," he called to her.

They were moving inshore now, scuffing past the rich baronial sea-going farms that dig their toes truculently into the Connecticut shore; and then he steered toward a large white motor cruiser at anchor. As he swung the seaskiff around the stern Leslie Cobb read "Gypsy 

New York."

He cut the switch. The seaskiff slid in a wash of water toward the cruiser's port side, crept closer, and he stood up to ease the seaskiff against the other hull. "What do you want, fellow?"

A young man with pimples on his sun-reddened face looked over the side at them. "Sh-h, brother. Come here." Johnny Smith searched his coat pockets, found a piece of paper and beckoned with it. The young man scowled suspiciously. "Did I say 'Come here'?" Johnny Smith scolded.

The young man leaned over a break in the cruiser's rail. "What you got?" he asked.

Johnny's elbow was bent short, as if he was rheumatic; and so the young man had to lean far down. And as he reached for the paper Johnny grabbed his coat sleevehe jerked hard.

The young man yelled "Say-!" He grasped the rail with his other hand but Johnny was pulling with both hands now, and the pimpled one fell into the seaskiff. 

Johnny jumped on top of him; the man was dazed by the fall; Leslie Cobb heard Johnny say, "Keep your mouth shut or I'll brain yuh," as he flourished an engine crank. The young man put his hand to the side of his head-he was bleeding-he lay in a ridiculously twisted position.

Johnny reached for the cruiser's scupper, swung over one of his funny bow legs, climbed aboard. She threw him the painter, he made it fast. He was somewhat hoarse as he called down to her-"Back in a minute, ma'am." He had a knife in his left hand. 

The seaskiff scraped against the cruiser. The pimpled man lay watching Leslie Cobb, who nodded. "Well," she said, "we meet again." 

He said, "Yeh?” "What did you three idiots intend to do with metaking me away in that car?” 

His face shifted in contempt. "Collect more ransom?" she persisted. 

He did not answer her. "Nice of you to throw me out that way. Didn't you stop to think you might have killed me?"

He jumped to his feet, the seaskiff rocked, he grabbed the painter and pulled the seaskiff back against the cruiser. 

As he started to climb aboard she said, "Sit down, please." "To hell with you." Being a Greek, he had no trouble putting sullen fury into it. "I said, sit down."

He looked over his shoulder in jeering anger, and paused, for she was holding a pistol in her lap; it was aimed approximately at his buttocks. "Say " he began. "Quiet; you nasty little creature. I'd shoot you except that I'm grateful for your intervening when that-what was his name? Smokie?-was strangling me”

He stood with a foot on the gunwhale, the rope in his hand. "Get down." 

He stepped down. "What's the big idea?" He kept his glowering eyes on the automatic pistol.

There were protestations from above. She saw two heads on the other side of the rail. One was Johnny's. 

The other belonged to a Greek who proved to be thin, hook-nosed, with collarless blue shirt, rolled sleeves and greasy corduroy pants. "Go on―" Johnny Smith was saying; "go over." He held his knife at the Greek's back. "Get down in there with that other cheap whiskey puller."

The hook-nosed man slid down to the seaskiff; he spoke quickly in Greek to the first man as he sat down. 

Untying the painter, Johnny called: "You didn't need to pull a gun on that mess of fried sandshark. He ain't got any guts."

The pimpled one put his thumb in his mouth, biting defiantly at Johnny, who climbed down to the seaskiff. "Well," he scratched his head, put his cap back on; "might as well get going." He started the engine, and as he bent down the hook-nosed Greek struck him with a short jab. Johnny fell and then the pimpled Greek dived on top of him. "Get back, you," Leslie Cobb cried, and then the flutter-

fingered young Greek came toward her, head lowered. "Get back, I said!" But he came on. She raised the pistol slowly, holding the seat with her left hand. "Will you get back?"

He was within two feet when she fired. 

He cried out, hand at face; he fell back, but the bullet had hit the water off to their right. The seaskiff now was scuttling unmanned out into the Sound, and then he came at her again. But she jumped up, balancing herself in the boat, and with a long reach of her arm she struck his ear with the flat of the gun. He reached for support on the gunwhale, missed, and fell over the side yelling. 

Johnny Smith heard his yells and looked up. He was straddling the hook-nosed Greek, his thumbs in the man's eyes. "Can you come here, ma'am? Now, just hold that gun on this fellow. We can't let that rat drown, he'd poison the fish.”

Johnny grabbed the wheel, swung the seaskiff in a skidding circle. As it overtook the man struggling in the water Johnny reversed the engine, leaned over, grabbed his shirt. "Hey, Nick, give a hand." 

The hook-nosed one, Nick, yelled something, leaned far out, helped pull in the young man, who rolled his eyes, choked, but was no longer flutter-fingered. They dragged him aboard. He lay on his stomach, coughing, groaning, the water running off him. But she didn't wait. She fired, then. 

Jim screamed, "Eha!"-dropped his revolver and hugged himself; then he fell on the deck, flopping like a beheaded chicken. Leslie Cobb turned to the rail. 

The pimple-faced Greek was climbing. She said: "Get back down there." He hesitated, swore at her. "I'll shoot you too,” she said. She could see the ugly splotches on his face, and the other man was imploring him to come down and holding one of his legs. 

Johnny Smith was at her side, pulling her arm. "Don't bother about them," he said. "All right,” she said. "Nice shot you made.”

Jim, the young Greek in the patent leathers, was on the deck; he was groveling on the deck. Johnny ran aft. "Come on!" he called, and she followed. She saw nobody on the deck. At a stairway he said, "Here! 

Give it to me." 

She gave him the pistol. "There's four left," she said.

He went down the steps, she was close behind. Her heel snagged in the small, steep step, and she caught the side of the companionway with her elbows. He was below, waiting for her, looking into a dark odorous hold, the pistol at his hip. "Dark as ditch water down here," he complained.

She broke past him, pushed into a room filled only with the reek of liquor. Some torn newspapers, some bottle straw and what looked like a discarded blue-denim jacket lay in the corner. ... "Vally ?" she cried. "Vally! Vally!... Where are you, Valery?"

She found another room, she opened the door, it was

black in there, the room sounded empty as she called in. "Valery. Valery. It's Mother, Valery.” 

Johnny Smith pulled her. "Come along here." They stumbled up a passage. They could hear her now. He grabbed the knob of a door near the foc's'le. It was locked. Leslie Cobb plucked at it and broke two fingernails, and he said, "You can't do it that way-" And he lunged at it, struck his shoulder and side against the locked door. 

Inside the child was whimpering. Leslie Cobb called to her, "It's Mother, Vally; it's Mother." And Johnny was swearing, he was striking against the door. "She can unlock it from inside," he panted. "Tell her to swing the catch from inside." 

Leslie Cobb said, "Unlock the door, dearest." They heard the child say, "I can't." Johnny ran aft, and returned with a chair. The passageway was narrow, so he had to stand with his back to the wall on the same side as the door, and he whipped the chair upward in a golf stroke, setting up a thunderous din. "Tell the little girl to get away from the door," he panted. "Stand back from the door, dearest."

Then a panel burst. And the chair broke at the same time. But he swung a chair leg and opened the panel. 

Leslie Cobb almost was struck as she rushed to the door. "Oh, darling," she said; "are you all right?" "Hold on" Johnny pulled her back. He reached in through the break in the door, fumbled. The brass lock clicked and he stumbled in. He brought the child out.

Leslie Cobb, stooping, pulled Valery against her body, feeling the girl's back, her neck, kissing her face and eyes, feeling her face with her fingers, kissing her. “Are

you all right, Vally? Tell Mother, did they-harm you?" She held the girl's face in her hands. 

Johnny dragged at Leslie Cobb. "Come on, or you'll lose her again!" He took the child in his arms and ran back toward the stairway. Just before he reached it he stopped. "Here," he said; "you take her up all right?" "Yes, yes."

He crouched up the steps, pistol ready, and they followed. There was no one on deck aft. Sun and wind greeted them. Leslie Cobb stared at Valery. "Why, what've you got on?"

The child was in a man's overalls. They were too large for her. Her face was burned brown. Her nose was crusted with burned skin. "He gave me these when my dress became so dirty," she said, and hugged Leslie 

Cobb. "Oh, Mother, I've missed you. Why didn't you come for me?" 

Johnny was saying something. Leslie Cobb asked: "What? What is it?" 

He said "By Harry!" and ran forward along the deck. 

Jim, the silk-shirt, patent-leather Greek she'd shot, was gone.

Ye gods!" 

Leslie Cobb was embracing Valery, smoothing back the child's hair. "I can't keep my hands off you.' "Wisht I'd knocked his block off," Johnny said. "Mother, they let me do the dishes. It was fun doing the dishes. In that room, there." 

Leslie Cobb asked her: "Where did they keep you, Vally? Here, on the boat?" "Yes, they did." "You've been here all week?" "Yes. The man came to the house. He said you sent for me." "But, dear, you know you should never talk to a stranger- 

"Yes, Blythe told me. But I forgot, and he took me in his car and we went to a house." 228

"What kind of house? Was it like a store? Who was there?" "Oh, some men." "No women? Or girls?" "No, Mother, and they told me I was all right and they showed me a lot of pictures-the most amusing looking men, Mother, they had on short skirts like ballet dancers, not like those Scottish soldiers in London, Mother. You know? That same night we went in a boat and we rode a ways and then they lifted me up in this big one. I tore my dress." "Th' skunks," Johnny said. "They're going in to 

Stamford harbor."

Leslie Cobb curled the child's hair around her forefinger. "But, Vally. Has the boat been right here all the time?" "Oh, no. We were sailing quite a bit." Valery gestured vaguely, taking in the hot haze, Port Jefferson, the wide part of the Sound, unseen Montauk Point and the vast open Atlantic beyond. "We went out and got some bags from a big ship but they wouldn't let me come out of that room downstairs; but I heard them carrying things from the big ship, and men talking loud; it was late at night. And next morning they let me out and there were just heaps and heaps of bags on our boat, and we came back. They called me "Tutz'." "Oh, Vally." Leslie Cobb gazed at her with tender melancholy. "You look like a ragamuffin." "I worried about you, Mother; but they were so nice to me. They played a mouth organ and sang the funniest music you ever heard." "Did any of those men touch you? I mean, put his hands on you, or— 

---

"Oh, no, Mother, they were awfully nice to me; they told me they were depressed as they needed money and they said you and Uncle John and Uncle Nev had a lot and when you gave them money they would bring me back home. I cried nights for you, though." 

Johnny Smith was stamping up and down, talking to himself. "We got to get off here some time,” he said.

Leslie Cobb got off her knees. There was a tremor in her throat. "Oh, Johnny, what do I care? Everything's tophole, Johnny. We've got Valery!" The sickness had left her face. She asked: "How am I going to tell you how grateful I am? Oh, I'm all choked up, I can't talk right, Johnny." "Mothers get that way," he said, and looked across the water. "The wind's coming up. You can't dope out this Sound in June, never could. Why, I remember- 99 

She seized his arm. "You've been a grand person!" "Forget it." "Johnny!" "Yes, ma'am?” "What can I do to show you how very, very grateful 

I am to you? What d'you want? Tell me what do you want most-in the world?" 

The little man turned his watery blue eyes to her. He was plucking his under-lip with thumb and finger. "Most in the world?" he asked. He looked like Will 

Rogers in a sheepish humor. "Yes," she said. "Want most?" he repeated and when she nodded he said, "You." "Oh, my gracious! What a fellow you are." "Well-you said most in the world."

She looked swiftly at him, at this little man, weather-

beaten, fiftyish, with his skipper's cap reaching no higher than her collar-bone, the ageing wrinkles under his eyes, and the large pores on both sides of his nose. It was an odd thing, but he seemed to smell of hay, of a warm hayloft; he was both naïve and enigmatic, like a boy; yes, like this inland sea with its deceptive surface placidity. "I know," he said unhappily; "It's my legs. Well, how 'bout second choice?" "All right." "Work for you. How's that? I've met a bag-full of women in my day, I can tell you; but, gee, you make them look like nine cents." He avoided her grateful silver eyes. "Listen-we got to get off this boat! They're coming back. See? How about me going overboard and swimming ashore? They's a house way off yonder by that point. It ain't so far." "But boats will be coming by," she exclaimed; "we can hail a boat. There's always boats out here." She shaded her eyes. Some sailing craft stood off Stamford light, miles away; a steamer was near the Long Island side; and somewhere there, on the shore, in vague profile, stood Sophia Pollard's Shorecrest-but you couldn't make it out from this distance, of course. The water was getting choppy and the wind blew her hair and billowed her light coat. The Sound was working into its famous mid-summer mood. "It's too far to swim," she said. "Wind is coming up from the south," he reflected. 

She cried impatiently, "It's so silly. We shouldn't have let them get away that way; but I was so happy I didn't care." She hugged Valery again. "That's what I figured," he said.

The summer storm came in a rush. The sky blackened, but over Long Island the sun still was bright and hot. Wind squealed through the rigging of the schooner, and Valery clung to her mother. All at once, large rain fell in thwacking splashes on the deck. 99 

Johnny Smith said: "Wait a minute, I got an idea.'

He still held the pistol. He gave it to her and went below. The woman and the girl sought shelter from the rain by flattening against the deckhouse. The rain smelled good after heat that blistered the deck. She saw some motorboats fleeing from the storm. The Sound gave the boats an exciting dusting off. 

Johnny came back shaking his head. "Thought I c'd get the auxiliary agoing," he said; "but it's dead. That's the way these booze pullers are, they can ruin a new ship in a week." He had to yell above the wind. “This ain't going to last. It's blowing over. You all right?”

She held her hair against her hat, nodded. The child huddled close to her and the wind howled down the rain. 

The schooner rolled, see-sawed, pulled at its anchor, dipped again. "Might as well go inside," Johnny shouted. He led the way.

From the wheelhouse they saw the motorboat approaching. The summer squall was moving northeast. 

Johnny said, "It ain't my boat. They're a-keeping my boat. By Harry, somebody's going to pay for that, let me tell you. I borrowed that boat, it ain't mine, and no Greeks-___" 

Flies bit him. He slapped his neck. "Don't worry. They're coming to take us off. Oh, it's silly-being marooned in Long Island Sound, of all places!”

They watched the motorboat come slashing toward the schooner. It had a soiled canvas awning. They could see several men aboard, and another at the wheel. "We'll stay here," Johnny said, looking over the wheelhouse. "Look, you sit there by the wall. Keep that gun hidden between you and the wall. Keep ahold of it." 

He blinked in thought. "Let me talk to them, first.” "All right, Johnny." "Mother, are they going to keep all of us on the ship?” "No, darling." "I'll show you how I washed the dishes."

The first man to enter the wheelhouse was Smokie with the big shoulders and choir-boy face. He had a short-muzzled nickle-plated revolver in his hand. He covered Johnny Smith. "Let's have yours," he said. Then he saw Leslie Cobb. "Well," he said, "if it ain't Gooseberry Eyes herself." He confronted Johnny: "Come ahn, you old poop, let's have it." "Who, me?” asked Johnny. "Let's have what?” "Gimme the gun, Oscar." “Listen, mister, I ain't got any gun," Johnny said. "You can search me."

Smokie was being crowded by men on the steps; they pushed in behind him. "Then you get it," Smokie said, raising his revolver. "Wait!" Leslie Cobb's voice was pitched low. "I have the gun. He has no gun. 

Nick, the hook-nosed Greek in the blue shirt, demanded of Smokie: "What the hell's going on?" 

A thick-set man demanded: "Yeah, what's it all about?" Leslie Cobb thought she recognized him as the driver of the Roton Point car, the one who had the

bright idea of throwing her out. He was the one they called Al. At his left stood the young Greek, Jim. Guess she hadn't hurt him much, after all. He looked at her with a peculiar glint in his black eyes. There was another man, a newcomer, who stood with his back to the left wall, a revolver hanging in his hand beside baggy knees. "So you got it?" Smokie asked. He had long eyelashes and a nice mouth. She nodded. "Well," he said, "let's have it." "No." "Didn't I say let's have it?" She shook her head a trifle, her chin high. "Want me to slap you down again, sister? Remember how I slapped you down in the car?" "I remember very well." "Let's have it; we ain't got all day." He moved toward her. There was a movement among the other men. 

Her eyes were stubborn and angry. "Now, see here. 

I've had enough of this; you've done enough. You surely must realize that all of you face prison for life—

The hook-nosed man snarled in Greek at Jim. Somebody else said, "Shut up, you!" But Jim screamed at

Nick, the hook-nosed man; at Smokie. He talked so violently that saliva began foaming at the corners of his mouth. The others jostled him. "Where's my money?" he shrieked. "You tell me take the kid for wedding money. Where's the money, my wedding money?" “Ah, shut up. Penelope wouldn't have nothing to do with a sap like you." 

Nick struggled with him, shouted at him in Greek.

Leslie Cobb felt a foot upon her left toe. She turned cautious eyes. Johnny Smith, on her left, was watching the quarrel; but his head seemed loose on his neck, it teetered toward the door. "I didn't get money. Why didn't I get my money?"

Jim clutched at Smokie's arms, unmindful of the nickleplated revolver against his stomach. "Crooks! Brigands! 

Eha! Son'bitches! Eha! Why'd you tell me she did not pay the twenty-five thou'?"

He pulled something from his pocket, backing away, his left hand plucked open the blade on a knife, he seemed seized with cramps, he crouched. 

Leslie Cobb leaned to Valery: "Sh'h, quiet now." She found the child's hand, she edged away from the wall seat, shoving the girl ahead of her; she worked around the men. And then Jim raised his knife. 

Somebody yelled: "Look out, Smokie!"

But Leslie Cobb was pushing Valery to the side near

the door, the child stumbled and Leslie Cobb jerked open the door; there was uproar behind them, and the child was rigid with terror, and Leslie Cobb half-dragged her down the three steps, leaving the door open for Johnny

Smith. She ran with Valery across the small deck, ran to the side where the motorboat was. 

Somebody dashed out the door, came up behind her, caught her from behind so she could not use her pistol.

His arms held hers, his hands cruel on her breasts, and he dragged her back from the rail. In the struggle his hands were forced up against her throat. She twisted halfway around, brought her elbow piston-fashion against his stomach. He grunted, pulled her down.

She freed her left hand and blindly reached for his face, his eyes; she felt flesh and dug her nails deep. The man drew back, swore at her, strained back to avoid her nails; but he held on, held her gun hand. Still she twisted, and when the chance came she brought up her right knee. Literally he wilted against her; she had to hold him off to smash his face with the pistol. Then she saw it was Nick, the hook-nosed one. He stumbled, fell on the deck.

She sank wearily against the ship's side, and Valery stood watching her. The child was paralyzed with terror; she shivered and stood there. "Where's Johnny?" 

Leslie Cobb turned toward the wheelhouse door. But

She tried to raise the pistol but her arm was numbed.

Al took the Colt.

face downward, his right arm doubled under his body, a knee drawn up as if he had been interrupted in prayer. "Oh-h-" She fell on her knees, drew the man's body over on his side, but he was heavy and she had to strain to move him. Johnny Smith's face had no expression; it was relaxed, the mouth a loose flap for yellow teeth.

There was a round clean hole where his right eye was, a hole from which blood slubbered and flowed down his cheek-red tears-and down on his chin, down on his collar and vest. She held him, and the blood dripped on her dress.

Inspector Moriarity, seated at his desk at Police Headquarters, faced the morning newspaper crowd. Some of the crowd's first editions went to bed at six that evening; so they were giving Big Mickey the rush act. "We've been looking for you since three, Inspector," said the American man. "What a pity, Frank," Inspector Moriarity said. He lighted a cigarette. It seemed incongruous for a big man with big hands to light a cigarette. "Maybe," he said, "I ought to stick here all day so's you boys can check with me every time somebody prints a wildcat extra about Roy Walters." "Have a heart, Inspector. What's the new developments?" "My men are making very good progress. There was a new development this afternoon but we won't know for sure until late tonight. You might give me a ring." 

The Daily News man was ingratiating. "Under the vest?" he said.

The inspector turned on him belligerently. "Under

the vest-we got a line on Sugarfoot's woman, and when that quick-triggered son of a bitch walks in tonight we're going to nail him like Jesus Christ got it. In due time, boys, you can print that." "Yeh? In due time for the P.M. sheets. Give us a break, Inspector." 

Inspector Moriarity smiled at the semi-circle. "I'm tired, boys; forgive me. You know Big Mickey's a regular guy. Here's something new. We've traced the jewelry he pinched off Sophia Pollard that night. Sixth

---

Tuesday A. M., June 16th

Leslie Cobb looked at her wrist watch. The lightningbug numerals said after midnight. Valery lay on the bunk beside her, face to the wall, and now and then she whimpered in her sleep. Leslie Cobb's body ached, every muscle seemed sore. Her nerves ached too. 

The anchor rope strained at its bitt, the old schooner snored and groaned in sleep. Leslie Cobb could hear the water's thup, thup, thup against the planking. She turned cautiously so as not to disturb the child; but her whole body protested with pain. The shoddy little cabin was hot. The only air came through a small transom. 

Her dress was wet with her perspiration. 

An hour later she sat up alert. A boat was puttering alongside, moving around the stern, splashing on the side where the woman and little girl were prisoners. Then the boat hit the schooner. A man cursed. Somebody talked back to him, tough. 

Leslie Cobb waited...

The key turned in the door, a flashlight entered. A man struck a match. It was, she saw, the big one, Smokie. 

He lighted the oil lamp swinging in its gimbel-ring on the wall. She stood facing him. "Well, Al," Smokie said, “she's still on tap."

Al and the hook-nosed Nick pushed in behind him. 

The man who had silently held a gun in the wheelhouse had not, apparently, returned with them. "You bet she's still on tap," Al said. "I was right out there. No broad can put it over on me, like Nick.” 

Nick said between partly shut lips: "Says you. She fight so hard as a man." His breath, Smokie's wet lips, showed they had been drinking back on shore.

Leslie Cobb drawled: "How long are you going to keep up this farce?... Put us ashore at once. I warn you that my family will, if necessary, spend unlimited thousands of dollars tracking you down and putting you in prison for the rest of your lives. If you are wise, you will put us ashore."

Smokie, liquored, looked more like a choir boy than ever. Almost any woman would have said he had a sweet smile. His lips were tinted for the Beatitudes. But his cigaretty voice sounded like a keg of nails being emptied. "Let's talk business," he said. "Your mother must be proud of you." 

He grinned to Al. "Gooseberry Eyes is still muscling in." "That won't get you anywhere," Al said to her. "You're splendid too," she said to Al. "Do you specialize on women and seven-year-old children?" "Only on women," Al guffawed. "I leave the kids to 

Nick here." "No! Jim," Nick said. He remarked to Leslie Cobb: “Jim, he was fine nursemaid for your kid all week. He needed wife bad too. Maybe he” "Shut up, Nick."

Smokie lighted a cigarette, tilting his head as he held the match. The cigarette waggled in the corner of his mouth as he said: "We've just been playmates-so far. 

See? Just a cute little fawncy society, haw-haw." He spoke faster: "Now, get this. We're in a hurry. We want the dough-re-me on the line." "If you remember," she said, "you delivered that tough-guy speech once before"-adding, "over the phone."

"Maybe you don't think we can get tough?” he asked. "O. K. with us. How about it, Al?" "You said it, Smokie." 

Nick, uninvited, said, “You bet!"

Smokie said to her: "We want you to write a letter to your brother, John. Tell him to give bearer twenty-five grand and say if he does anything to bearer he don't get any more loving letters from his little sister. Tell him if he has bearer tailed we don't return his sister Or maybe we'll just keep the kid, eh, Al?" · • · “Yeh,” Al said and added, "We got to get what it takes. 

We know what you knocked down from Sophia Pollard.

We was talking of asking fifty, your brother gets a bargain." "Hey, Nick, you got that writing paper I told you?" 

Valery woke up. She squinted at the lamp. "Mother,

I'm thirsty." 

Nick said: "Here's the box. I got fountain pen here in my pocket, wait a minute.” "Come on, let's have it." 

But Leslie Cobb refused the stationery or pen. "I won't write it,” she said, and put her arm around Valery. “Oh, no?” "No." 

Smokie said: "Like to see us work on your kid? First thing we do hey, Al, how about you hiking up to the galley and heating the potato masher?" 

Al grinned, started for the door. "Sure," he said. "We'll pretend we're cowboys, we'll brand a calf for you.' 

Leslie Cobb managed to pitch her voice low, but she could not control the unevenness. "There's no decency in you. Even the most incorrigible scoundrels, I've heard,

have a code of honor. You haven't that. I gave you the money you asked; you kept my daughter and attempted to carry me away too. That is the way you are. How would I know-if I did write that letter, and I am not saying that I will!-that you would live up to your side of the bargain this time? Would set us free?" "Sister, you ain't in any position to call us names," Al said. "Write the letter," Smokie said; "we get the twentyfive on the line and I promise you and the kid go ashore O. K." "You 'promise'?" Her laugh was ugly. "That's it." 

Suddenly she said: "I'll do it. But on condition that the money is delivered to you after my daughter and I are in Stamford. It must be after-not before."

Smokie's pretty mouth twisted incredulously. "Now isn't that a good idea!" he said and raised his hand as if to strike her. "Listen. Who's doing the double-crossing around here? Us? Says you." He appealed to Al and 

Nick. "Didn't she agree to lay off the cops? Yes. And when we pick you up"-he said to her-"who signals the dicks to put the wood on us, eh?". "Yeh," Al said. "And makes me wreck a swell can.

If that was my can I'd make you pay for it plenty." 

Their faces were greasy with sweat. Their sweat and the whiskey they'd been drinking made the cabin unbearable. 

She asked: "Why did you try to kidnap me too? I gave you your money. Why didn't you return my daughter ?" "That's our idea. We got lots of ideas. If you hadn't tried to fox us with those dicks you and the kid'd be

home now-and we'd of collected the fifty grand from 

Brother John. Yeh. But what do you do? You try to give us a good foxing with those dicks.” "The high society crap," Al said. 

All this was unreal, stagey. She said: "I'm still afraid you don't know what an awful fix you're in—' 

Smokie said, "We're in?" "Yes. And you don't realize how smart you'll be to set us on shore without any more of this nonsense. You may have forgotten, but I haven't-you killed Johnny Smith."

Smokie's face tightened. He drew back his right elbow; but Al and Nick grabbed him. Al's forehead wrinkled in pleading. "Lay off that," he said to her. “He didn't kill that old guy. That old guy shot himself. I seen him shoot himself. Didn't you, Nick?"

The hook-nosed Greek was excitably positive. But she insisted: "You shot him down in cold blood. It was a beastly thing to do.”

Smokie grasped Valery's arm and Leslie Cobb pushed him off and with his left hand he palmed her on the chin. Valery was screaming between them. Then Nick and Al caught Leslie Cobb's arms; she kicked back at them; they pulled her away from the child and Smokie picked the girl up and moved toward the door. 

His face was misshapen with malice. "We ain't getting nowhere here. So I guess the kid and me has got to go out for a smoke. Come on, tutz." 

Valery, still screaming, kicked his shins, writhed. "Mother, don't let them... Mother... !" "Come on, tutz," Smokie said. "A nice little girl like you shouldn't have to listen to such language from its 

Ma. Come on!" He dragged the child outside, and was

gone several minutes. When he came in the door they could hear Valery screaming for her mother. Amused, he leaned against the wall. "Now, Gooseberry Eyes," he said; "we got more room to talk in.-Don't wrassle that way, you'll get hurt. Al will hurt you. Take it easy, that's the babe."

Struggling between the two men Leslie Cobb flung at him-"You goddamned swine-you swine-you swine." "Come on, you birds." Smokie nodded toward the door. "We'll let Gooseberry Eyes stay in here for a while and cool off. Maybe when we start working on the kid she'll play ball."

The three men went out. She heard the lock turn. 

She was out of breath, her face was wet. She sat on the lower bunk. Her head sank lower, her body slouched. 

She sobbed painfully into her hands... 

Tuesday A. M., June 16th

It was about 3 A. M. when they came back. She had pounded on the door, called out repeatedly; but not until three did they show themselves. Smokie's shirt was off; he was wearing trousers and B. V. D.'s. Nick had a handkerchief tied around his neck. Al wiped his face. 

His shirt was off too. "Sister, I do need jack now. Nick plays poker, what I mean! You know how Greeks gamble." Smokie held out a friendly bottle. "Want a shot?"

She shook her head, did not look up. "Take a shot. It's good tiger sweat." He pushed her shoulder. "Go on-take a shot." He was fairly well drunk. She pushed him away. "Oh, yeah?" he said and turned to Al and Nick. "Listen, yous; who's got the brains around this yacht?" He pronounced it "yachit.”

"Al, I got a good idea, listen, Al. We don't work on the kid yet, see? I don't want to hurt the kid unless we got to. I'm soft-hearted. You ask anybody and they'll say, 'Who him? He's soft-hearted,' they'll say. Dam' if I ain't!"

He pulled the nickle-plated revolver from his front right pocket and negligently covered Leslie Cobb. "This's what you get if you don't act reasonable. I'm going to show the boys how I get them to write letters." 

She faced him, numbly defiant, and he pushed her back on the bunk, slipped, fell heavily upon her. "Dirtybeast-" she said but the words were muffled; she threw him to one side as she slid off the bunk and fell, her knees jutting up, in a sitting position on the floor. 

Al laughed and asked Smokie what was his idea, softhearted.

She was on her feet facing them, breathing fast, and then she was at the door. She pulled it open and was almost out when Al grabbed her dress, Nick caught her wrist. Al and Nick pulled her back in the room. 

Smokie held the revolver in his hand as if he were spraying with a water nozzle; he said: “Bring her back in here." They brought her back; he put his face close to hers. "It's all in the spirit of fun, see? We're just a couple of big business men." He paused; then he reached forward, his left hand touched her. "Ah-you-you-!" She kicked out at him and he announced to the others: "Gooseberry Eyes's got a million dollar shape." She bit her mouth in pain as the two men twisted her arms. Smokie sidled up. “I like a big diz," he said. "A good big diz is scarce." He hurt her with his fingers. His choir-boy face was livid with sweat. 

He slipped both arms around her and snarled at the

other two: "Let go her," and as they released her arms he whirled her around. They might have been waltzing. 

But he shifted to the right, pivoted her on his left hipshe struck madly at him, and slid heavily to the floor, striking her head. He lost his balance and fell on her. 

The other two were at Leslie Cobb's head, holding her shoulders and arms to the floor; they slobbered in drunken laughter, and Nick pulled open the top of her dress.

Smokie now was on his knees beside her right leg. He looked up at the other two in slow fury, lifted his revolver-"What's the idea?" he asked. Nick said something. "Yeah?" Smokie said. "Well, I can handle her myself. I don't need no help, Nick. You two guys beat it." They demurred sullenly, wanted to know how he got that way.

He leveled the revolver at their faces. "I said beat it. You going or want to have it?”

Leslie Cobb, dazed by the fall, opened her eyes to see the two men reluctantly going out the door-Smokie's head turned watching them, his revolver ready. "After me," he said. “Get that, you heels? After me.' 

As she rolled on her right side she brought her bent knee in a swinging arc against him, and she struck his throat with her tight fist. He seemed to cough and fall, and she was on her knees, she pushed him down with beating forearms and he fell back, his head striking the wooden shelf of the lower bunk, and then she had his revolver. She stood over him, an insanity in her eyes; but on her lips there was a smile as she pulled the trigger. 

She shot him twice in the groin. The revolver clicked empty-she faced the door, screamed at the bull-necked 

Al: "Put your hands up. Put your hands up, I say- "9 He stood bewildered, mouth open. She advanced on him, he backed into the passage, collided with Nick.

"You too!" she said to Nick. They raised their hands. "Go forward." Hands upraised, they shuffled ahead.

The door near the foc's'le was locked. "Unlock that door," she said. They looked stupidly at her. She cocked the trigger. Al took a key out of his trousers' pocket, unlocked the door. She said: "Come on, dearest; come quickly." 

Valery walked out between the two men. Leslie Cobb said: "Now-walk backward, walk aft, and keep your hands up, but I'd much prefer to kill you, too-" As they moved past her and the girl, Leslie Cobb flattened against the partition, moved quickly, and put the two men between her and the stairway. "Arms up," she said. "The first arm down is the signal to shoot." She urged Valery on ahead of her. "Keep on to the stairs," she said.

The two men had trouble getting up the steep stairs backwards. They slipped, scuffed heels. Once Al, who was ahead, slipped and in falling he struck Nick. They grabbed the sides of the stairway, caught themselves, and remembered to jerk up their arms. Valery burst out laughing. "They're so funny, Mother," she said. "Aren't they?" Leslie Cobb said.

On deck she made them stand with their backs to the stairway. "I think," she said, "I'll shoot you both back down. That would amuse my daughter immensely." “Give us a break," Nick pleaded. He hunched his shoulders, wrinkled his face in pleading. He looked like 

Mr. Punch with his hook nose, his grimacing face. "I'll think it over," she said. "Go over to the rail."

The light was beginning to break in the east; the wind was damp and chill. She ordered the two men over the side, and helped Valery with her left hand. Nick reached up from the motorboat and lifted the child to a

seat. Leslie Cobb climbed down and sat with Valery in the stern. "All right-Stamford." 

Nick, miserable, turned around. "Eh?" "Stamford." 

Al said, "Ah, I don't know how to run this boat." "All right. Then row us." 99 But Nick, bending shakily over the engine, managed to start it. The engine roared, frightened Valery. Leslie 

Cobb drew the child's head against her shoulder, said: "Don't try anything, you see? Or you'll get what your friend's just got, back there.”

The motorboat, Nick at the wheel, shot ahead; swung across the schooner's bows, romped in a thrilling spread of water toward shore. "Oh, gosh, it don't look 

Her mother sighed, kept 

Valery, looking back, said: like such a big boat, does it?" her eyes and the revolver on the two men up ahead. "It's so nice and cool, Mother," the child said. "It was dreadfully hot where they put me."

She crept into her mother's arms but Leslie Cobb was grinding her teeth, and trembling; it seemed as if she were fighting off an impulse to faint. It was Valery, clinging to her neck, whimpering, that roused her. "Dear God!" Leslie Cobb sighed, and kissed Valery.

They came ashore on the Stamford waterfront at a coal dock not far from Gus Papageorge's house on the bedraggled terrace. Leslie Cobb stepped off first, drew 

Valery with her. She waved the revolver angrily. “Go on, get out. What're you waiting for?"

Al temporized, standing in the boat, both hands on the gunwale. "What're you going do with us?"

"Don't know yet. Let you know later. If," she said, "I feel as I do now I'll kill you both. Get out!"

They came off the boat. Again she told them to raise their hands, then the four walked across the small wooden wharf and came out to a deserted street, the two men without shirts, the tear-stained girl whose huge overalls dragged her heels, the tall woman with unkempt hair and blackish stains on the front of her dress. 

They walked two blocks. A man came by in a car and 

Leslie Cobb called to him but the man sped past. She said to the two men: "Keep walking. If either of you move I'll shoot-and how I hope you move."

They marched for five blocks more. Stamford was asleep. There were dingy stores on both sides of the street, and a car line; but they met neither owl cars nor human beings... Then there was a sputter far off, but it came closer and she saw a motorcycle swing around a curve in the street.

It was coming toward them. 

She shifted the gun to her left hand, waved the other hand and cried, "Stop; stop, please stop.'

The cycle policeman swerved over toward them from the middle of the street. He braked so hard he had to drag his toes on the pavement to keep his balance. And 

Leslie Cobb was suddenly unnerved, she was crying incoherently at him—and then Al ducked his head, started running. He lifted his thick legs like a bicycle rider, he ran a zig-zag course as if expecting to be fired on. "Oh, get him, get him." And she ran up behind Nick, she shoved the dead gun into his back. 

The motorcycle cop drew his revolver as he scrambled from his machine, which fell on its side. He fired as he ran.

"I ain't with those bad fellas," Nick said. "I'm just poor sailor, I got wife, t'ree kids. Let me go, Mrs., please, you let me go?"

She could see the side of his face. She had scratched him good and plenty. He pleaded harder. "Let me go 

Mrs., please!" 

His arms became tired, she jabbed him. "Keep them up! Now answer me truthfully and I'll you whether I'll let you go-we'll see if tell the truth." "I got t'ree kid, my wife is sick—" you can tell "Tell me, did Peter Papageorge have anything to do with this business—the kidnapping of my daughter-our detention on that boat?" "Pete? Oh, no, Mrs. No Pete. Pete, he is just bootlegger-" He tried to look at her over his shoulder, the gun muzzle made him grunt. "Pete wouldn't do anything so bad." 

Valery touched her elbow. "I'm so dreadfully sleepy," she said. "Yes, dearest. My own lovely dearest in overalls. 

You'll be back in your own bed very soon." Her left arm caught the child in a terrific hug. "Now, lady," Nick said, “you let me go? Eh?"

The motorcycle cop was coming up the sidewalk with Al, whose forehead was bleeding. His lips were puffing up. The cop held a bracelet on Al's wrist. 

Leslie Cobb gave Nick a shove. "I said I'd tell youif you told me the truth. I think you haven't. That's just too bad. When you sit in the electric chair you ought to tell the priest how very sorry you are." "You, punk-" the motorcycle cop said; "come here."

Nick hesitated and the cop, dragging Al along, struck

Nick across the bridge of his nose. He locked Nick's wrist into the other end of Al's bracelet, said to Leslie 

Cobb: "Now, what's it all about?” 

She handed him the revolver. He snapped it open, caught empty shells in his hand. He looked grinning at 

Al and Nick. "Dog-gone if it ain't shot out!" he said. "That's good," he said to her. She was tired, awf'ly tired, but she managed to grin. 

Later, from Police Headquarters at Atlantic Square,

Leslie Cobb and Valery were driven in a police Pierce to their home on Newfield road. They were followed by a taxicab. 

Miss Blythe, for an Englishwoman, was quite emotional. "Put what we need in some bags," Leslie Cobb said. "The taxi's waiting." "I say, aren't we staying here?" Miss Blythe looked up in surprise. She was kneeling on the floor, holding Valery. "Not a chance," Leslie Cobb said—"we're going direct in to New York. The country's too much for me. I need a rest." 

She ran to the top of the stairs. "Oh, Sundquist. Are those officers still down there? Will you please ask them the name of the motorcycle man who helped me?"

Thursday A. M., June 18th

The policewoman was a large, stylishly dressed woman named Mary Keane. She was about forty-five, wellpreserved, used rouge, had a stunning head of black bobbed hair, and called Leslie Cobb "dearie."

"Well!" she said, rising from the table two waiters had placed for her and the nurse. "I'll never reduce on this kind of food. It's the best breakfast I've indulged in since the week Queen Marie was in New York. The 

Queen didn't have any time for me, though; she liked the big young cops; she went soft every time a Paddy helped her out of the car.... Why, yes; I like this work. 

You move around, that's what makes it interesting. We get so many juvenile delinquency cases nowadays. Very sad. Little girls of twelve, fourteen, fifteen and up. wouldn't believe me if I told you some of the things. 

Oh, yes, dearie; I'm a widow. My husband Mike was killed in '25 chasing some young stick-up men through

Thursday P. M., June 18th

John Phelps came with his wife Ann. They were on their way to dinner at Central Park Casino.

In the twenty years of her married life Ann had not, deliberately, changed the mode of her wardrobe. She wore new things, expensive things; but they always seemed up-state. She "abhorred showy jewelry." Her lips looked thin merely because she was so modest with her lipstick. She was eminently part of the righteous ensembles who sponsor charity-benefit pictures for the 

New York Times roto section. 

Leslie Cobb bantered John. "How are you going to face the disgrace of your sister's playing hostess to a policewoman with pocket-gun and a shield? . . . I'm sorry." She waved a lovely arm toward a Miss Shulberg. "Miss Shulberg Mrs. Phelps; Mr. Phelps." She said to John: "I have a new chaperone from Police Headquarters every eight hours. Lost interest in sleuthing? You were going strong for a while." 

She wiggled contentedly under the silk coverlet. "You are right, I'm through. I've had a bellyful.”

Ann's expression didn't change; but somehow she managed to disapprove Leslie Cobb's English. "I've had a bellyful," said Leslie Cobb maliciously.

Avenue shop as did Herbert Hoover. Ever since a Washington oil lobbyist told him about it, John too ripped open the boxes and tossed the cigars into a desk drawersmoked cigars as if they were cigarettes. It gave him a cough that worried Ann quite a bit.

Friday A. M., June 19th 

Mrs. Keane, revived after knocking off a breakfast of black Belgian grapes, shredded wheat and cream, double lamb chops, bacon and lamb kidneys, Melba toast ("They say it's so good for dieting!") and coffee, got up from the table and walked unaided into the living room. "Miss Seiler," Leslie Cobb said to the nurse, “since the Police Department in three shifts is building up its hips on my money, won't you be good enough to find out where these women go? For instance, where's this person going?" "I thought you knew, Mrs. Cobb. There's always a detective loafing out there in the hall. They go out for a little chat." "My God, I must be a dangerous criminal."

She waited for Mrs. Keane to return. But this morning Mrs. Keane returned in a hurry. She was bursting with news, among other things. "What do you know about that! They've taken Sugarfoot Walters!" "H'm . yes?" • .. "Oh, it was thrilling!" "How do you know?” 

Mrs. Keane was ready for any skeptic. "Frank Walsh just told me. He's on the detail with me. He was just talking with one of the boys at Headquarters. Oh, boy, how I'd love to've been there." "Been where?" "Why, heavens above, there were a couple hundred police squads surrounding the building, Frank said-it was up on the West Side somewhere-and riflemen firing from the roofs all around, and this Sugarfoot and his buddy and some woman barricaded—Yes, trapped inside

a flat on the top floor; and they used smoke bombs and tear-gas bombs. And the battle was something fierce!

Monday A. M., June 22nd

John Phelps had offices on the fourteenth floor of a large building on lower Broaway near Bowling Green. 

Leslie Cobb, in the lobby, read the directory and found he had rooms 1401, 1403 and 1405. She took an elevator. 

The reception girl's desk was partitioned waist-high from a room containing typists and men at desks. "I should like to see Mr. Phelps." "Who'll I tell him is here, please?" "Mrs. Cobb." 

The girl looked her over with new interest. "Oh, yes,

Mrs. Cobb." She reached for a plug in the switchboard. 

After a moment she looked up. "Mr. Phelps is not in, but his secretary will be right out. Won't you take a chair?" 

Nodding toward the stenographers, Leslie Cobb said: "All those typewriters! Don't you mind them?" "No, we're used to it." 

Leslie Cobb stood at the rail; she looked at the typewriters. Her left arm was in a black silk sling tied behind her neck.... Then a middle-aged woman, immaculately dressed, with lustrous grey hair and silver pince-nez held by a silver chain at her waist, came up to her. "Mrs. Cobb? Mrs. Leslie Cobb? You are Mr. Phelps' sister, of course?" "Yes. Is he in? I shan't take but a few minutes." "I'm sorry, Mrs. Cobb. Mr. Phelps went across the street to the S.O. building. He might be several hours." "Well, I'll wait a little while." "Oh, surely. Won't you come in his office? I think you'll find it better, waiting there."

John Phelps had a large office, substantial in furnishing, overlooking Broadway. Leslie Cobb saw the flocks of pigeons fluttering on the sculptured eaves of the Customs House. The secretary gave her a chair. She looked through a copy of National Petroleum News. 

After a while Leslie Cobb stood beside the woman, who was busy at a steel file cabinet. "I wonder,” she hesitated, "if while I am waiting for my brother I mightn't write a letter?" The woman was gazing at the arm-sling. "But I can type with one hand," Leslie 

Cobb said. "Are you going to use your machine?" "Oh, Mrs. Cobb, of course you may. Do you want some office paper, or plain?" "Plain. That will do nicely." 

Presently the secretary smiled ingratiatingly and said: "I'm afraid, Mrs. Cobb, you wouldn't make your living as a steno-not working just a right hand!" 

Leslie Cobb said woefully: "I can't use these machines.” “Oh, I think they're lovely, Mrs. Cobb. They're so lovely and quiet." "Haven't you any other about?" "Well," the secretary scratched into her lustrous grey hair with her pencil; "there's an old portable Mr. Phelps carries with him when he's traveling to the fields. Like to try that?" "I'm putting you to so much trouble." “Oh, not at all, Mrs. Cobb."

She went into a closet and returned with a portable machine in a black case. "Here," she said. "I'll put it on this table for you, it may be more comfortable. 

Personally, I hate the nasty little things. They're like flivvers."

"Do you?" Leslie Cobb said. She took the sheet from the big machine, sat down, rolled the sheet into the portable, wrote with her forefinger: 

Mrs Cob If you are as smart as you think you are youll

She stopped, leaned forward to read what she'd written. Then, slowly, she took the sheet out of the carriage, crumpled it as if impatient with her effort. She dropped the crumpled sheet on the table and took another sheet. 

She tapped the typewriter keys for some time. Then she said: "May I have an envelope? And an air-mail stamp . . . Thank you so much." 

She addressed the envelope, licked flap and stamp, picked up her gloves. "I'm afraid John will be too long," she said. "You'll tell him I was here?"

When the secretary went over to take the portable away, after Leslie Cobb left, the table had only the unused sheets of paper. The secretary looked in the wastebasket, she even opened up several crumpled sheets that she knew she herself had thrown in; but she could not find the crumpled one Leslie Cobb had discarded. 

The red Down light flashed above an elevator door.

Leslie Cobb went to take it and then a door clanked open on the other side and John Phelps stepped out. He turned to the right, walked rapidly. But the Down car stopped, two doors opened. "Never mind!" she said to the operator and hurried down the hall. "Oh, John. John!" 

He stopped, turned around. "Oh, hello, Leslie. 

What're you doing downtown? Spoors told me you'd be in bed for a week." He eyed the black arm-sling.

She said: "Came to get my steamer tickets. Cunard's only a few doors up, so I dropped in to see you. Being as Valery and I are going back to England to liveshe hesitated, smiling-"thought I'd chat with my brother for a minute or two. We probably won't see one another soon again." "No," he said rather heavily. "Guess that's so." "Miss me?" 

He chuckled. "Why, surely I will, Leslie. We get along all right, don't we? Won't you be wanted for the trials of Walters and these Stamford kidnappers?" "I suppose I'll be compelled to make a flying trip back in September, but I won't stay any longer than I can help." She looked away, read "Oil Syndicate, Ltd." on a frosted glass door, nodded. “Yes, John, we get along all right. But after the way I talked-back at my housebefore you, Neville and Ricks-I'm afraid youseemed too wrought up to go on. She

His large heavy face was bland, heavily good-humored. "I see what you're driving at. No apologies, Leslie. 

Just forget it, old socks. Anybody's liable to fly off the handle in a tough situation like that." He seemed to fall back on oil-men's English when he became stirred. 

People were in the hall. "Leslie, come on to my office." "No. Thanks. I'm rather shaky on my legs, I'm going back to the hotel. Feel the heat more than I thought I would." She hesitated. "I-I'd like to ask you a question, John. You don't have to answer if you're going to lie."

Didn't you?" With a gasp-as if the thought became reality for the first time-she added: "Yes, and you've had those detectives shadowing me. You were afraid

Wryly, "No, guess not."

He said: "I didn't know it was in my little sister-this marvelous sleuthing ability. How do you do it?" "An average success, John. But I'm a poor showman.

A professional detective-to judge from those I've met lately-stage their deductions with a grand flourish. I just blurt mine out." 

Amused, he said: “All right, I did it. And I'll answer that other one. 'Why?' Because, Leslie, at the time you were the only one who seemed to know about Aunt's murder-it was rather a trying time for me right then in business-I knew what it'd be if that broke loose. 

And I didn't want the family dragged into the bloody mess either. And I figured she was dead and gone, so what the hell? . . . Guess I was pretty heartless about it, Leslie-heartless toward you and toward Aunt, who,

after all, has left us all some darn nice pin money. I shouldn't have done it. I know that now."

As they talked men and women, and now and then a messenger boy, passed them; but the conversation was so intensely low-pitched that no one appeared to hear what the talk was about. 

John Phelps watched a girl with pretty legs go toward the elevators. "But at the time," he said, "I was in a panic over everything. And I thought that by some crudely written letters I could frighten you into packing up and beating it. . . "Detectives?"

He wheeled on her. Passions were dark in his large meaty face. "All right," he said, muscles tensing on his jaws. "I did it!" Perspiration was limp on the front of his collar. She did not move, or speak. He jeered, “I tell you I did it!" She leaned against the marble wall. "What of it?" he asked.

Annoyed, she rose, went to the door in the foyer, opened it, slammed it shut. 

Then the Countess Tatiana Pflomm appeared.

She had on a chiffon negligee that clung to her body. 

She looked as if she had just emerged from another of her frequent baths. Her black hair was caught back of her ears. Quietly she said: "Why did you say you were Margery?”

Her flat, meowlish voice droned: "You were not so well off a few weeks ago. Ah, I know; Neville has told me." 

He said steadily: "Told you what?" "How things were with you-your business. Not so good, eh? You thought-ch?-the end of the world had come? And where was the money coming from to save you-and now you ask me to save you, but not with money; you have much money now." She moved close, looked up at his eyes; she did not seem to notice that he held her. "Eh?" she said. Her teeth were even and white, her skin was white. Her lips were alizarin-red. 

He moved to kiss her, she pushed him back. "Eh?"

Her voice might have belonged to a Boulogne fishwife, snarling in the market by the quays.

"I know—and only I!" she was saying. “And that is why I hate you, you see? Because you tried to put it on poor Neville's shoulders." "What are you talking about?” "As if you did not know? Eh, Mr. Phelps?... And I did not either-until all I read in the newspapers. Some thief that is what they say-descends upon your Aunt and shoots her. Not? And all day I weep in terrorweep for my poor, innocent Neville. That poor boy. 

Yes, I weep and wait in terror; for did you not come to his car that Sunday afternoon? I was amongst the flowers near her garage, near the flowerhouse. I saw you but you did not know. And you took the pistol from inside the door. That was the pistol used to kill your 

Aunt but you thought they would say, 'How sad, it is 

Neville's pistol!' Not?" .. · 

She put a sly tongue on her lips. "His other one-but the same calibre-I made him give me, I was afraid of you but I tell him I am afraid of the American gangsters. ... And I waited, I pleaded with God to save my Neville -save him from his brother. Are you not a nice brother? To sacrifice your brother for dollar selfishness?"

She walked over to a table, dragged off a red and white homespun embroidered by Russian peasants, threw it over her shoulders. "-Eh?" 

At last he said, "I'd rather "Yes?" you knew than anyone else." "Yes," he said. "Because now I've got a real argument for this deal between you and me! You've got to say yes now; it's your only salvation. Hell, you don't think I'd let you be, with all that? No, Tatiana-no. You and 

I are going away together-we'll live high, wide and handsome all over the world-you and me, Tatiana; forever."

She hissed through small white teeth. 

He spoke slowly-"If you don't” "You kill me too. Eh? Like you kill your old Auntlike you would have had Neville die for your crime?"

She moved her head toward the bedroom. "Like you tried to kill your sister, eh? ... Oh, Neville tells me every-thing. He was not fooled, no. How you threw her from your yacht because she was digging with her miserable fingernails into your secret. I hate her-like you do, but I tell you this to show you what I know of you. 

Tch! Swine!" She spat into his face. "Christ-!" He came sullenly. "If you touch me Neville will kill you." She backed away, half-crouching. "Get out, please. Get out!" She turned and ran to the bedroom and pushed the door into Leslie Cobb; she clawed at the door until she had it open. 

John Phelps stopped when he saw his sister. "Do you know," she said, "you had me completely buffaloed this morning? You did, John. You were cleverer than I gave you credit for. . . . And yet, I should've guessed you'd come traipsing in here. Ann gave you away.' "Ann?" 

"In her nice way." She addressed the Russian, who was hiding somewhere in the bedroom: "He had me believing he really did!-that Neville did it. Ah, well!” 

John Phelps said: "You're in at the finish, anyway,' and added: "As a red-hot detective, you're lousy." "I'm lousy." "9 "You could take lessons from that sweet little Russian in there." "I could, at that." "Well... "Is she?" 

I suppose Ann's wondering where I am."

He stopped, came up to her. "Remember, Leslie, what 

I told you this morning? That's still correct!" "What is?" "I said: Aunt's dead and gone, cremated. Even her dust's gone now. There's not a whit of evidence against me-nothing-just the say-so of two wild-eyed females. 

I'm-" he hesitated. "I'm sorry now; it'll mean living purgatory for me, I'm afraid, I wouldn't do it again to own all the goddamned oil wells from Baku to Kettleman 

Hills.... Lord, I'm tired! I'm going to cash in my chips, maybe take Tatiana there-oh, yes, I will!-to Europe, or 

Samoa. Leslie, I'm a bum poker player. I'm too soft.

Way it looks, Leslie, this Sugarfoot Walters will have to" -he was laughing now; he laughed until he had to lean against a table-"have to take my hand-sit in for me.” 

She flinched. "You would let this man die?" "He shot your detective, didn't he?" He asked angrily, "Say, what are you doing up here, anyway?” 

"Want me to tell you? I-honestly!—came up to entrap Neville for Aunt's murder. Isn't that funny? Didn't know how I was going to do it. That's funny too... 

Abruptly she said: "I talked to Walters today. He's a narcotic-the police have some hold on him, or else he was so narcotized Sunday night he believes he shot Aunt." "He's crazy. We surprised one another. He must have been coming from the house-nice little scrap. He shot me in the arm." Her eyes widened. He said: "You're remembering that 'boil,' aren't you? This fellow got my gun away from me. Guess he would've let me have it right-then old Plunkett came running. Scared him. 

Lucky for me, wasn't it?” "Hell!" she said. "What does one do with a brother like you?"

CHAPTER XVII THE STOWAWAY 

Saturday noon, June 27th

THEY walked out on the boat deck. Hardly anybody was on the boat deck. They looked down at the hubbub on the pier. "Lean over, 'way over," Leslie Cobb said. "See those portholes down at the water line-by those orange peels?" 

He said: "I see the orange peels and a chicken crate floating around." "Well," she said, "that's about where we three werecoming over. Third. I was broke. That, my dear, dear 

Ricks, is why you failed to find my name on the firstclass list, when you suspected me-oh! and so cruelly, so inconsiderately” "Go on-it's coming to me."

The excitement around the Mauretania was too much for Valery. Miss Blythe noticed her uneasy tip-toeing; she took the child inside. "Gosh, Leslie; gosh, Leslie"

Boys were running up the gangways with late boxes of flowers, baskets of fruit, telegrams; people stumbled down the inclines. A whistle bellowed. "There's the All Ashore, Ricks." "Wish we were going together!" "Do Ricks? You're a sweet guy." you, "-Give my right arm to be going across with you- 99 309

"Well..." Her under-lip trembled. "Come along! 

Save the right arm to put around widows happening to be rather heart-sick and quite miserable— "What d'you mean?” "Come along-if you wish.' 

Somebody, down there, was bawling: "All-1 ashore as is go-ing a-shore! . 

Ricks Whittle pulled her around to face him. A middle-aged man and his stout wife watched. A girl with a camera stared. "Mean that?” "Uh-huh." "But-" he protested; "I've got no things-no passport- 99 "Can get cabin. It's not full, I shouldn't think. You could go as far as Southampton, Ricks . . . There'd be four days . . . and five nights . . ."

The whistle was bellowing until it shook the deck under their feet. 

Gangplanks rose skyward toward them.
